# Marketing Math & Unit Economics

LTGP:CAC, contribution margin, payback windows, forecasting, and the math that decides budgets.

Part of the [[00-Codex|Advertising Science Codex]]. Claims follow the tier system (T1 docs, T2 shown test, T3 practitioner, T4 theory).

## Claims

## LTGP:CAC and the Death Spiral

### MM-001 · Average first-order contribution margin for e-com brands went from +$9 (2013) to -$29 (2022 report); giants died the same way
Tier: T2 · Status: active
A 2022 industry report cited: in 2013 the average e-commerce brand made $9 contribution margin on a first-time customer; by 2022 the average brand was losing $29 per new customer. SmileDirectClub IPO'd at $8.9B and was liquidated 4 years later; Casper spent $423M on advertising in 4 years while still losing about $300 per mattress. All collapse back to the LTGP:CAC equation, not "creative problems" or "bad quarters".
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Death Spiral That Kills Most eCommerce Brands, 2026-07-22
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-002 · The LTGP:CAC bands are real but the ratio is not portable: three operators state their numerator out loud and they do not agree
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Below 1 you lose on first purchase, only viable with exceptional retention, which 99.9% do not have. At 1-2, $1 in ads returns $1.50 gross profit, leaving too little for opex unless extremely lean or at enormous volume. 2-3 is where budgets get ramped; above 3 you are leaving money on the table and should push spend harder. Benchmarks come from 1,000+ brand audits.

Restated across three further videos with the measurement window made explicit. The default window is 180 days. 30-day and 90-day windows are fine for benchmarking a business against itself, but comparisons across industries need a longer window because CPG and fashion carry different retention profiles. Below 1 is restated as a CAC payback period worse than 6 months, which is a cash-flow statement rather than a profit statement, and he names it as his red line: he would not run or work with a business at that payback without funding. He also warns that sub-1 is usually just weak acquisition efficiency dressed up as an LTV strategy. Why the ratio and not acquisition MER: LTGP:CAC is comparable across businesses and across time, while MER targets depend on gross margin profile and channel mix. Conversion: at $100 first-purchase gross profit, the bottom of the optimal band means a $50 CAC.

Contested on the numbers by Mark Builds Brands, who sets 3:1 as the bare minimum LTV:CAC and pushes high-ticket to 7:1 or 10:1, a band that MM-002 would read as chronic underspend. The likely reconciliation is the numerator: Blue Sense divides lifetime gross PROFIT by CAC, Mark divides lifetime REVENUE by CAC, so a 3:1 LTV:CAC on a 50% gross margin product is only 1.5 LTGP:CAC and sits below the scale zone. Neither side showed comparative data.

The numerator question got its answer on 2026-08-20, and the answer is that operators genuinely differ. Three of them state their numerator and they do not agree, so the ratio is not a portable number and the argument above has no single correct side.

Nick Theriot uses REVENUE, pre-COGS, and says so explicitly, twice, on screen: "this is gross profit, but like way before like cost of goods, pre-AOV, and stuff like that. So, keep in mind it doesn't have that part right there." His live account read is revenue over spend, $135,000 spent for about $247,000 in revenue at a 1.82 ROAS and a $53.49 cost per acquisition. His 12-month cohort is customer SPEND throughout: "their first order with us is $35... over on month 12, they have a customer LTV of $132. $62 to acquire a customer." Gross profit is never mentioned anywhere in the walkthrough. Carry the warning: anyone quoting Theriot's LTV or LTV:CAC figures as gross-profit-based is misreading him, and a 12-month LTV of $132 against a $62 CAC is a 2.1 on revenue, not a 2.1 on gross profit.

Andrew Faris uses GROSS PROFIT. His ratio applies gross margin first, 60 points in the worked example, "cuz now we're considering the CAC and the margin in the business", and he keeps a standalone LTV column that is total revenue and deliberately does not fold it into the ratio.

Blue Sense uses GROSS PROFIT at CART level. He states no ratio at all in the unit-economics transcript, but blended CAC of $58.50 is subtracted from cart-level gross profit to reach a profit contribution of $16.76, so gross profit is the quantity CAC is netted against. A guest on the same channel, Angus, goes back the other way and names customer LTV, a revenue figure, as what licenses a higher CAC: "they know their customer LTV is $500 or whatever it's going to be based on their subscription. So they know they can spend more on CAC."

Do not resolve this to one numerator. The finding IS the operating rule: state which numerator a ratio uses before acting on it and before comparing it to anyone else's, because a 2.5 on revenue and a 2.5 on gross profit are different businesses, and at 50 points of margin they are a factor of two apart. It also reframes the Mark Builds Brands disagreement above. His 3:1 minimum may not be a different opinion about scaling at all, only the same opinion expressed on a revenue numerator.

First worked example with the numerator named, banked 2026-08-19. On a roughly $2M/year Shopify brand, blended CAC reached $118 in March and was tracking toward $210, while first-purchase GROSS PROFIT was $60 to $70. He computes the ratio on screen as 0.48 and calls it a business that goes bankrupt inside 60 to 90 days if held. That is the Blue Sense numerator used explicitly, on a real dated account, with the figures shown. It is now one of three stated numerators rather than the only one on file. The same session restates the band, calling anything above 3 outstanding, which matches the top of the scale zone above. Full autopsy at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-146|MM-146]].

The contesting operator's fuller ladder, banked 2026-08-21, and it confirms the numerator reading above rather than settling anything. In a second video Mark Builds Brands states three linked rules: average order value at least 2x front-end price, lifetime value at least 3x AOV, and customer acquisition cost under 50% of AOV. The last one sets roughly a 2x ROAS floor before gross margin is applied, and he says it should be adjusted by gross margin, which is the closest he comes to the gross-profit numerator. He names CAC as "the single most important metric in this entire video" and concedes the AOV rule is not consensus: "my KPI here once again is very aggressive and a lot of people don't agree with it". All ASSERTED, no account shown. Note that LTV at least 3x AOV plus CAC under 50% of AOV implies an LTV:CAC of at least 6, which is double the 3:1 minimum he states elsewhere, so his own two statements do not reconcile without knowing which numerator each uses. That is the claim above demonstrated on a single operator rather than across three.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Death Spiral That Kills Most eCommerce Brands, 2026-07-22; Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, The 1 Bottleneck I See in 80% of eCommerce Audits, 2026-05-18; Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21; Blue Sense Digital, Be Careful: TikTok Ads Nearly Bankrupted This Business, 2025-03-24; Nick Theriot, Best Meta Ads Columns Setup for Ecommerce (2026), 2026-07-03; Nick Theriot, Every Level of Scaling an ECOM Brand ($0, $30k, $100k, $1m, $10m), 2026-06-10; Andrew Faris, A DEEP Dive On How Retention Works For Ecommerce Businesses, 2026-04-21; Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01; Blue Sense Digital, Stop Sending Cold Traffic on Meta to PDPs, 2025-10-30; Mark Builds Brands, how to turn any product into a money printer w branded dropshipping, 2026-02-17
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-003 · Whether e-commerce must be profitable on the first purchase
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Blue Sense Digital holds that e-commerce specifically must be profitable or at least liquid on first purchase because consumer repeat rates are weak, unlike B2B and agency models that break even at month 4-6; sub-1 front-end economics can only be funded by repeat-revenue subsidy, investor capital, or debt. Nick Theriot holds the opposite for LTV categories: supplement brands normally scale at 0.8-1.5x front-end ROAS with returning-customer revenue carrying profitability, and needing 3-4x front-end signals a margin problem, not an ad problem. Fraser Cottrell leans the same way as Theriot for subscription brands, warning in-platform ROAS understates their true value (see MM-015). The resolution likely depends on real, measured retention: without proven cohort LTV, the Blue Sense rule is the safe one.

A worked case for the Blue Sense side, reported by Andrew Faris. She's Birdie built a software product specifically to manufacture back-end LTV on a low-repeat physical product. The software failed, the brand reverted to a one-time-purchase business, and the whole marketing calculus reset to make-money-on-first-purchase. Faris puts the consequence at roughly twice the MER the business had been running at, later hedged to maybe 50% better by the Q4 point, and at drastically lower spend. That is the cost of reversing into first-order profitability after betting on LTV that never arrived.

Professor Charley T lands on the Theriot side with an explicit procedure for the "we cannot wait for LTV" objection: set the break-even window equal to the measured repeat-purchase window. If 20% of customers repurchase within 90 days, run break-even across those 90 days, and from day 91 the repeat revenue arrives on top of a break-even acquisition engine, so the business runs at roughly 20% profit with acquisition cost unchanged. The procedure is only as good as the repeat-window measurement behind it, which is exactly what [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-006|MM-006]] insists on and what the Blue Sense side says most brands do not have. Worked reasoning, no account data shown.

Blue Sense applied his own first-purchase rule to a live channel decision on 2025-07-24, which dates it and shows the arithmetic instead of stating the principle. A 21-day Google Demand Gen geo holdout implied a $140 incremental CAC on new customers. He names the escape hatch himself, that a CAC payback period would make $140 work, and then refuses it: on first-purchase acquisition this is not profitable and the exchange was bad. The campaign was kept at a much reduced budget and reclassified as returning-customer retargeting rather than acquisition. It is the first instance in this file of the first-purchase rule being used to kill an acquisition thesis rather than asserted in the abstract. The holdout itself belongs to the Google and attribution files.

The Theriot side finally has account figures behind it, banked 2026-08-20, and they are read off screen rather than asserted. Client cohort one: first order $35, $62 to acquire, 12-month LTV $132, so "we don't break even on customer acquisition to month two" and months 3 to 12 are the profit. Client cohort two: a first-time-customer ROAS of 0.79, an explicit month-one loss, against a $235 LTV that carries the cohort profitable across 12 months. Two caveats hold the claim where it was. Both figures are REVENUE, pre-COGS, per the numerator finding in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-002|MM-002]], so the true payback month is later than month two once product cost is netted out. And a modelled 12-month LTV is a forecast, not a banked figure, which is the precise thing the Blue Sense side says most brands do not actually have.

Ben Heath adds the category boundary that stops this being read as an e-commerce-only argument: whole categories run loss-making first-purchase acquisition and recoup over months or years. "Almost anything finance-related. Banks, they pay way more to acquire you than they make from you initially." Mortgage and insurance the same. He names cash reserves as the structural advantage large advertisers hold there, which is the honest reading of the whole contest: sub-1 front-end economics is a financing position, not a marketing position, and it is only available to an operator who can fund the gap. See also [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-155|MM-155]], where Taylor Holiday's data read pushes even high-LTV brands back toward first-purchase efficiency.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Death Spiral That Kills Most eCommerce Brands, 2026-07-22; Nick Theriot, I spend $100k a day on Facebook Ads (here's who will scale & fail in 2027), 2026-08-14; Andrew Faris (with Richie Mashiko), From A 65% Decline To All-Time Revenue & Profit Highs, 2026-04-27; Professor Charley T, How to Scale Profits with Meta Ads, 2026-01-24; Blue Sense Digital, We Tested Google Demand Gen - Here's What Happened, 2025-07-24; Nick Theriot, Every Level of Scaling an ECOM Brand ($0, $30k, $100k, $1m, $10m), 2026-06-10; Ben Heath, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026, 2026-02-20
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-004 · The death spiral is a feedback loop: CAC creeps up, fewer customers on fixed budget, revenue falls, spend raised to hold revenue, CAC rises further
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Without repeat-customer revenue holding the top line, a CAC creep (e.g. $50 to $60) forces spend increases that inflate CAC further; brands arrive at audit 4 months in with CAC 2x and LTGP:CAC below 1. He reports this exact cohort pattern in roughly 100 of his 1,000+ audits. Teams misdiagnose it as a traffic, creative, or seasonality problem; the diagnosis always starts from CAC and LTGP tracked monthly, with every structural change (new agency, new platform, new product) timestamped against them.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Death Spiral That Kills Most eCommerce Brands, 2026-07-22
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-005 · Split the P&L into first-time vs returning customer to unmask losses: a business can "make $100k/mo" while losing $70k/mo on new customers subsidized by repeats
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Filter every P&L line by Shopify's order-type tag into a first-time-customer P&L and a returning-customer P&L. Example pattern: first-time contribution slid from +$20k to -$70k/month while returning customers contributed +$300k, so the blended P&L looked healthy and masked the acquisition collapse. Sub-1 LTGP:CAC can only be funded three ways: repeat-revenue subsidy, investor capital, or debt.

The same split runs one level down, inside the unit-economics deck rather than the P&L, and the same operator insists on it there: "we're splitting the unit economics of first time returning out we're separating them because it's really important to understand the dynamic in isolation." In the account he walks through, the two cohorts move in OPPOSITE directions on discount rate and gross margin, which a blended deck cannot show. The figures are at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-158|MM-158]].
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Death Spiral That Kills Most eCommerce Brands, 2026-07-22; Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-006 · Measure retention only via cohort analysis tied to that month's CAC, never via Klaviyo-attributed revenue, returning-customer %, or daily repeat-rate snapshots
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Read the Shopify cohort table: month-0 base revenue per customer, then day-30/60/90 columns show cohort revenue growth. Pull the 90-day column, convert to gross profit with real COGS (not an assumed %), and divide by that month's CAC for a 90-day LTGP:CAC. Brands that KPI CAC flat while ignoring the LTGP side miss month-to-month first-order GP swings (e.g. $70 in August vs $40 in November from portfolio mix and discounting) that flip the same $50 CAC from profitable to loss-making.

Two construction rules from Andrew Faris that decide whether the table is readable at all. Discard month zero, meaning customers who bought a second time inside the same month as their first purchase: "it's usually a small number. There's a lot of noise in it, so I just don't really worry about it." Start the retention read at month one. And run conditional formatting VERTICALLY, comparing each month-N column against itself across cohorts: "this conditional formatting is only vertical. This is really really key... So each month is compared to itself." Formatting across the row compares month one against month six and produces a gradient that means nothing, which is the fastest way to read a cohort table backwards.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Death Spiral That Kills Most eCommerce Brands, 2026-07-22; Andrew Faris, A DEEP Dive On How Retention Works For Ecommerce Businesses, 2026-04-21
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-007 · Industry structurally sets your LTGP band: fashion ~55% GP lift by day 365, CPG ~110%, furniture ~zero; tactics cannot move you out of the band
Tier: T3 · Status: active
About 80% of businesses have no meaningful lifetime gross profit contribution (maybe 20-40% lift by day 365). Furniture is near-zero repeat (purchases tied to moving house every 3-5 years), so those clients are managed purely on first-purchase GP with CAC held flat. Retention benchmarks to KPI against: fashion ~55% and CPG ~110% cumulative GP lift by day 365. Set CAC targets from the industry leaders' realistic band, not from aspiration; a $50 creatine product cannot tactic its way to outsized LTGP.

Fashion gets a second checkpoint at day 90: good fashion brands see about +15% gross profit lift from existing customers by day 90 and about +55% by day 365. Index fashion on the 180-day window ([[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-002|MM-002]]) because GP growth inside the first 180 days varies heavily brand to brand and the window has to be long enough to contain the return event. A brand below the +15%/+55% band has to fix front-end acquisition efficiency, because the retention band will not be tactic-ed into compliance.

Second category example of a structurally acquisition-led business: Fashionphile at roughly $400M revenue sells an average of 1.1 items per person per year. The founder says they actively want to retain buyers and cannot, because a ~$2,000 average selling price caps affordability and the goods last 10-15 years. Purchase interval is a property of the category, so the business runs permanently on new-customer acquisition plus referral rather than lifecycle marketing. Read alongside [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-030|MM-030]]: no email or SMS execution moves a purchase interval set by the product.

The same structural limit shows up BELOW the category, at SKU level, which is where it becomes actionable. Entry analyses on real brands have turned up products and categories running a 2 to 3% repeat purchase rate. At that rate every one of those customers has to be profitable on the first purchase or never, so the SKU either carries a much tighter efficiency target than the rest of the catalogue or comes off spend entirely. Run it as the other half of the hero-SKU query in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-079|MM-079]]: the analysis that finds what to advertise also finds what to stop advertising. Observed across an onboarding book, no per-brand figures shown.
**PRECISION PROBLEM on the fashion day-365 figure, found 2026-08-29, and it needs resolving before the band is quoted again.** This claim banks fashion at ~55% cumulative gross-profit lift by day 365, with a day-90 checkpoint of ~15%. In a June 2025 video the SAME operator states the fashion band as "90-day growth ... at about the 15% mark" and "at 365 ... around the 45% mark". **The day-90 figure replicates exactly. The day-365 figure does not: 45% there against 55% here.** He is loose about it in the second telling, calling them "relatively broad index metrics" and "generalized ballpark figures", and the 45% is the number he then runs his worked scaling arithmetic on at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-184|MM-184]]. Neither figure is sourced to a dataset in either video. **Quote fashion day-365 as roughly 45 to 55% from one operator's recall, never as 55%, until a shown cohort curve settles it.** The CPG ~110% figure is restated unchanged in the second video and is unaffected.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Death Spiral That Kills Most eCommerce Brands, 2026-07-22; Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25; Andrew Faris, Fashionphile's 27 Year Journey To $400M, 2026-05-29; Blue Sense Digital, Best Google Ad Account Structure for eCommerce in 2025, 2025-09-02; Blue Sense Digital, Where To Allocate Agency Fees On The P&L, 2025-06-10
Last touched: 2026-08-29

### MM-008 · For sub-$300k/mo brands the killer is often opex bloat forcing debt for inventory, a cash-flow death rather than an LTGP:CAC death
Tier: T3 · Status: active
From working with roughly 300-400 SMB e-commerce brands: inexperienced capital allocators over-invest in operating expenses, compressing margin, forcing debt to fund future inventory buys, and the business dies of cash constraint even when the unit economics could have worked. The same warning applies at international expansion: duplicated inventory across two markets plus localized creative spend strains working capital, and multi-country expansion 9x's the diagnostic workload.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Death Spiral That Kills Most eCommerce Brands, 2026-07-22; Blue Sense Digital, The US Launch Playbook for Non-US Brands, 2026-08-17
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-009 · Over-optimizing conversion via dark patterns or overstated angles lowers CAC now but destroys LTGP later through returns, chargebacks, and dead repeat rates
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Persuasion tactics and claims the product cannot fulfill (e.g. "firmer skin in 4 weeks" when untrue, or scaling an eyesight angle a supplement cannot deliver) produce a short-term conversion lift that shows up as higher return rates, chargebacks, lower repeat rates, and weaker 365-day LTV. LTV must be tracked per angle because product efficacy differs by the promise being scaled.

Second operator, generalizing the same failure from claims to the OFFER. An offer strategy that looked acceptable on its acquisition metrics produced cohorts that performed materially worse than expected afterwards, and the damage was visible only by tracking cohort behaviour past the purchase. No cohort curve, no lifetime-value figure and no window are given, so nothing here is a size. The transferable rule is the routing one: judge an offer change on post-purchase cohort behaviour, never on the acquisition metric that the change was designed to move. An offer that buys a better CAC is guilty until the cohort clears, which is the same standard this claim already applies to angles. The instrument for doing it is the cohort table in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-006|MM-006]].
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, eCommerce CRO Masterclass 2026: The Full System, 2026-08-10; Andrew Faris, Build A Culture Of Truth-Telling In Your Business, 2026-05-22
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-071 · Accidentally spending 20-40% of budget on existing customers builds a $24-30 CAC into the one cohort that should have cost nothing
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Returning customers carry zero acquisition cost, higher AOV and higher units per transaction. Spend leaking onto them creates a large CAC on that cohort, because repeat volume is small so a modest absolute spend divides into few orders. It also wastes the one structural advantage DTC has, which is owned first-party data driving free repurchase. Unit economics were SHOWN for a real brand: $51 first-time-customer profit contribution per order, with the returning-customer column materially higher on both AOV and units per transaction. The 20-40% leakage share is ASSERTED ("everyone almost does" it unless the account is stringent about exclusions), so the claim is tiered T3 on that half. Operator action: exclude purchasers from prospecting, then check what share of spend still reaches them before trusting any blended efficiency number. Extends the split-P&L discipline in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-005|MM-005]].

Boundary added 2026-08-19, from the same agency, and it stops this claim being read as "never spend on existing customers". A 21-day Google Demand Gen geo holdout measured a 6.8% period-on-period lift in returning-customer orders in the test states while the rest of the country dipped, 1,521 achieved against 1,211 expected, at $39 per returning-customer order, which he calls very profitable and keeps running as a declared retargeting campaign at reduced budget. That does not conflict with the leakage above once the difference is named. Leaked spend inside a prospecting campaign is unmeasured, unintended, and priced into a CAC nobody computed. A measured incremental lift on the returning cohort at a known cost per order is a purchase you chose. The test that separates them is whether you can state the incremental lift and the cost per order for that cohort. If you cannot, it is leakage.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How To Structure Your Meta Ads for Profit (Free Live Webinar), 2025-02-19; Blue Sense Digital, We Tested Google Demand Gen - Here's What Happened, 2025-07-24
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-072 · Returns double effective CAC in fashion: at a 50% return rate a $50 CAC against $100 first-purchase gross profit is really a $100 CAC
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The standard calculation (AOV $200 x 50% GP = $100 GP, so target a $50 CAC for a 2:1) silently assumes every acquired customer keeps the product. Half return and never buy again, so the surviving customers carry the full acquisition cost and the ratio falls from 2 to 1. Second correction on the same line: state whether the gross margin percentage is inclusive or exclusive of markdown sales, because fashion brands routinely liquidate unsold stock on a sale page, and stock that is donated or destroyed still has to be charged against average GP. Both corrections push fashion toward a longer measurement window so refunds land inside it, which is the reason for the 180-day default in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-002|MM-002]].

Third statement of the same correction, now placed inside the unit deck rather than the ratio: "if you don't include this, particularly if you're in fashion and you have 5 to 15% return rates which is pretty typical, is that you'll run your unit deck, it'll look profitable, but there won't be a consideration of the really high return rates that actually take you to zero or negative profit." Return rate belongs on the cart line, not in a footnote.

Flag the spread rather than averaging it. The same speaker uses a 50% fashion return rate to illustrate the CAC doubling above, "5 to 15% pretty typical" in the unit deck, and a 10% standard fashion return allowance in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-064|MM-064]]. Those are three different numbers for one category, so none of them is a benchmark. Pull your own return rate from your own refunds before running any of this arithmetic; the transferable part is that the line must exist, not what goes in it.

The return rate also moves with geography, which is the part that turns this from an accounting note into a market-entry rule. Fashion brands launching into the United States see return rates 50% to 100% higher than at home, and some clients run a 3x refund rate in the US against their home market. He attributes it to consumer behaviour rather than to the offer or the product. The phrasing on the 3x is loose about direction, so record it as a reported multiple rather than a measured one, and note that a 50-100% lift and a 3x lift are not the same statement. The operating consequence is specific: the refund allowance has to be built into that region's marketing KPIs before the region gets its own efficiency target, or the US campaign is graded against a home-market unit deck it cannot hit. Country-level unit economics is [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-166|MM-166]].
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25; Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01; Blue Sense Digital, How To Breakdown Shopify Data Into Country Unit Economics with Valentin, 2025-07-22
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-073 · November/December cohorts return more and retain worse, so an identical acquisition MER in BFCM is worth materially less than in January
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Three causes: discount-driven buyers, gifting, and generous return policies that invite buy-ten-keep-two behaviour. He reports seeing brands in international markets hit 80-100% return rates during discount periods, which means effectively all revenue refunded and front-end efficiency of zero. Consequence: a 4x acquisition MER in January and a 4x in November are not the same result, and the November target must be set above January's by whatever the expected return-rate and LTV delta is. Set that number before the period, by back-propagating from the net profit target through opex to a contribution target, then to the required revenue and efficiency at the volume inventory allows. ASSERTED from fashion client work, no cohort export shown.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-146 · A 50% media increase bought a 6% net sales increase and drove LTGP:CAC to 0.48: the fullest additive-channel autopsy in the codex
Tier: T2 · Status: active
A roughly $2M/year Shopify brand scaled TikTok from $600 to $10,500 a month as purely ADDITIVE budget, on top of Meta and Google rather than reallocated from them. Total media went $40,000 to $60,000 a month, a 50% increase, and net sales rose 6% period over period. Blended CAC reached $118 in March against a run rate tracking toward $210, while first-purchase gross profit was $60 to $70, so LTGP:CAC computed live on screen at 0.48. His verdict was 60 to 90 days from bankruptcy if held. Shopify's period comparison, the blended CAC chart and the cohort read are all on screen; this is the first shown, dated, real-account demonstration of the [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-002|MM-002]] ratio in the file, and it uses GROSS PROFIT as the numerator explicitly, which is evidence on MM-002's contested definition.

Three readings to carry. First, the marginal-efficiency ratio itself: 50% more media for 6% more net sales is a worked instance of diminishing returns on additive channel spend, and the arithmetic is worse than it sounds. Taking the $2M annual figure at face value, roughly $167k a month, a 6% lift is about $10k of extra net sales bought with $20k of extra spend, so the incremental money did not return its own cost even at a 100% margin. That is our arithmetic off his stated figures, not a number he computed. Second, the order-count trap: orders rose substantially while average order value fell, so an operator reading order volume alone would have called the channel a success while it was diluting the very AOV that sets the affordable CAC. Third, the sequencing error that produced it, which is that the budget was purely additive rather than tested against a holdout, so the only measurable question left was whether the whole business got better, and it did not.

The rest of the autopsy is split across the codex by topic. How the platform's own numbers created the false signal is in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-067|MM-067]]. The traffic-quality instrument is [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-147|MM-147]]. The TikTok delivery mechanics and the cross-platform attribution gap belong to the TikTok and attribution files.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Be Careful: TikTok Ads Nearly Bankrupted This Business, 2025-03-24
Last touched: 2026-08-19

## Finance Fundamentals: Margin, CAC, and P&L Targets

### MM-064 · Product margin and gross margin routinely differ by 40+ points: a $100 tee at $30 COGS is 70% product margin and 24% gross margin
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Cost of delivery has to include product cost, import duties, inbound freight amortised per unit, outbound shipping ($11), pick and pack ($4), transaction fees (3%), flow-through tax ($9), and a refund/discount allowance ($15 at a standard 10% fashion return rate). That is $46 of extra variable cost on the $30 unit, so $76 cost of delivery and 24% gross margin. Named error: transaction fees parked in opex because they feel fixed, when they vary with revenue and belong in cost of delivery. This matters because the margin number a brand hands its agency sets the marketing allocation and the ROAS target. At a genuine 70% you can spend 20-25% of revenue on marketing. At 24% you are driving the product at a loss and nobody in the account knows it. Terminology trap to fix in every conversation: CM1 = product margin, CM2 = gross margin, CM3 = contribution margin, and speakers routinely swap them. Illustrative worked example, ASSERTED, not an exported P&L. Feeds [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-010|MM-010]].

The mirror-image error, and he demonstrates it only to refuse it: dividing operating expenses by order count to get a per-order opex figure, $4.84 in the account he shows. "I don't like this at all because operating expenses are fixed and shouldn't scale proportionate to volume and therefore this is a poor exercise, but you can do it, I see people do it all the time." Put together with the transaction-fee error above, the test is directional rather than definitional: a cost that moves with revenue belongs in cost of delivery even when it feels fixed, and a cost that does not move with volume stays out of the unit deck even when a per-order number would be convenient. A per-order opex figure makes volume look like it fixes a cost problem when it does not.

Band on the transaction-fee line, from the same operator, replacing the flat 3% used in the worked example above. Payment processing runs 2.5% to 3.5% clean, closer to 4% to 5% once foreign exchange conversion is in the path, and 5% to 6.5% once buy-now-pay-later is in the mix. Practitioner benchmark range, no data behind it. The spread matters because the top of it is more than double the bottom, so a brand selling cross-border with BNPL enabled carries roughly 3 extra points of variable cost against the 3% assumption, which comes straight off contribution margin and therefore off the affordable CAC. Pull your own effective rate from the payout statements rather than using any of these.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04; Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01; Blue Sense Digital, How To Breakdown Shopify Data Into Country Unit Economics with Valentin, 2025-07-22
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-065 · Tag COGS in Shopify instead of assuming a gross margin percentage, because discounting compresses margin non-linearly: 20% off a $100 product at $50 COGS drops GM from 50% to 37.5%
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Price falls, unit cost does not, so a 20% discount takes 12.5 points off gross margin, a roughly 25-30% relative collapse. Brands hand their agency an averaged margin percentage from the accountant, and that single number gets baked into profit contribution, LTGP:CAC and every dashboard. It is wrong exactly when it matters most, during Black Friday and promo windows. The fix is mechanical: tag cost of goods on every product in Shopify so real-time gross margin flows into any external dashboard or sheet and captures discount compression as it happens. This is the practical precondition for every finance-grade metric downstream and the concrete implementation behind the assumed-percentage warning in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-006|MM-006]].
**The account-level consequence, from the dedicated episode on this, and it is the number that makes the claim worth enforcing.** Baseline month: $100,000 of revenue, cost of goods assumed at 30% = $30,000, shipping and fulfilment at order level = $10,000, transaction fees at 3% = $3,000, so gross profit is $57,000 and, after $20,000 of ad spend, contribution is **$37,000**. Now discount 30%: the $100 product at $30 cost was making $70 a unit and now makes $40, and holding revenue flat at $100,000 means moving far more units to get there.

With cost of goods actually tagged, Shopify returns **$55,000** rather than the assumed $30,000, fulfilment rises with the order count to $15,000, transaction fees stay at $3,000 because revenue did not move, gross profit falls to $27,000 and contribution lands at **$7,000**. **With the static 30% assumption the model returns $37,000 for that same month. The true figure is $7,000, so the model overstates contribution by 81%.** That is exactly the "80% or 90% lower" he describes, and it is the gap that appears when the accountant's profit and loss statement arrives two months later and contradicts the agency's November report. Every figure in the chain recomputes.

Two more things ship with it. **Accuracy band:** tagged cost of goods will still not be dollar-for-dollar with the eventual statement, but he puts it within **5% to 10%**, against being wrong by 80% to 90% on the assumption. **The alternative is not practical:** recomputing a fresh cost-of-goods percentage for a promotional month requires forecasting sell-through at individual product and bundle level, which he says almost nobody can do and which introduces its own error.

**The operating payoff is a live KPI, not a reporting improvement.** With cost of goods tagged you can pull gross margin for yesterday, see it compressed by 20%, and move the efficiency target from 4 to 6 or 7 the same day, so the marketing goal tracks the profit the discount actually leaves behind. That converts this from a bookkeeping chore into the input that sets the number the media team is held to.

Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, The 1 Bottleneck I See in 80% of eCommerce Audits, 2026-05-18; Blue Sense Digital, Why You Need To Have COGS Tagged Up In Shopify, 2025-09-03
Last touched: 2026-09-05

### MM-066 · KPI paid media on acquisition MER (new-customer revenue / total ad spend); blended MER is a CFO metric and a bad marketing KPI
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Same formula as MER with the numerator cut to new-customer revenue. SHOWN with an onboarded client's January data: platform ROAS 2.14 against an acquisition MER of 1.41, from $79k attributed revenue against $56k first-time-customer net revenue. The failure blended MER produces, reported twice: MER reads 4x in January and 5x in February, so the team spends more in March, while acquisition MER underneath ran 2.0, then 1.8, then 1.6. February's lift came entirely from returning customers, and the extra spend eroded acquisition further. A second worked pair: a 2.0 blended MER that is a 1.4 acquisition MER. Hygiene rules attached. Use NET revenue with returns deducted, which is acute in fashion where today's gross revenue compresses later. Cut every finance-grade metric to new customers only (acquisition MER, new-customer profit contribution, new-customer revenue, cohort size). Acquisition MER tracks tightly with Meta 7-day-click ROAS, which is the argument for reporting on that window. There is no universal good acquisition MER, because the target is a function of gross margin profile and retention.

Track a second version alongside it, acquisition MER adjusted, with returns added back. Refunds get processed in batches, so clearing a backlog on one day drops net new-customer revenue and puts a cliff in the chart that has nothing to do with delivery. When adjusted hits target and unadjusted misses, the problem is returns, and the follow-up is finding which products drove them. Neither metric exists in standard tooling; he built both as custom metrics.

Where acquisition MER itself breaks: once other channels drive new-customer revenue (his example, $1M/month of influencer spend), the ratio credits Meta with revenue it did not create. Blended MER stays legitimate as a CFO control on what share of the P&L goes to marketing. Related to [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-005|MM-005]], which splits the P&L; this is the ratio metric and the reconciliation test between platform reporting and the bank account.

Second independent operator adopting the same instrument, banked 2026-08-20. Andrew Faris uses acquisition MER as the column that reconciles a retention read back to value created, and calls the correlation between aMER and value creation very strong. The failure mode he models with it is the one this file keeps meeting from other directions: a brand reads high retention rates, gets ad-spend happy, starts acquiring at a 0.9 aMER, and finds by month six that the retention advantage was never large enough to fund the CAC. Honesty flag, and it is the important half: the sheet he demonstrates this on is FABRICATED and he says so on the record, so the 0.9 and the correlation are illustration, not measurement. What is real is that a second agency running $10M+ clients independently settled on acquisition MER as its standing control metric.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How To Structure Your Meta Ads for Profit (Free Live Webinar), 2025-02-19; Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04; Blue Sense Digital, The 1 Bottleneck I See in 80% of eCommerce Audits, 2026-05-18; Andrew Faris, A DEEP Dive On How Retention Works For Ecommerce Businesses, 2026-04-21
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-067 · True CAC is total ad spend across all channels divided by total new customers; channel-level CAC is not calculable
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Platform CPA runs off attributed conversions and always flatters the real number, so optimising to in-platform CPA means optimising to a number the bank account never sees. Compute CAC daily, weekly or monthly from the business's own new-customer count against all ad spend everywhere. Splitting CAC by channel reintroduces the attribution problem the metric exists to escape. CAC alone means nothing without gross profit on first purchase beside it: a $100 CAC against $10,000 GP is excellent, a $100 CAC against $20 GP loses $80 per customer.

Shown case of the flattery, and of what trusting it costs. On a roughly $2M/year Shopify brand, the TikTok account reported 1,100 conversions against 158 seven-day-click purchases, because most campaigns were optimising for add to cart and some for view content, so page loads were being counted as conversions. Dividing spend by that inflated conversion count and setting it against a first-purchase gross profit of $85 to $90 produced an apparent LTGP:CAC of 7 to 8, which the incumbent agency read as a scaling signal and ramped spend against. Computed on purchases the real figure was 0.48 ([[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-146|MM-146]]). Rule that falls out, and it is the cheapest check in this file: before believing an outstanding ratio on a new channel, look at which EVENT the platform counted in the denominator. An optimisation event that is not a purchase inflates the conversion count and deflates the CAC in the same step, so the error compounds rather than cancels.

The small-and-mid-sized-business version, from Michael Nadalin: drop the attribution suite entirely and read spend response at the account total. Pull the raw data out of Shopify and each ad platform, put total spend beside total revenue, and look for correlation between spend increases and result increases. His argument for dropping the tool is that every suite ships several attribution models and whichever one you select changes the answer, so the model choice is arbitrary. He adds that his own clients' Triple Whale views make his results look better than his blended reads and he does not believe the better numbers. Scoped explicitly to businesses small enough that the cause-and-effect chain is short. Reasoning, no test shown. The rigorous version of the same read is the daily-spend-against-acquisition-MER regression in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-077|MM-077]]. Blue Sense puts an upper bound on that method independently: simple before-and-after correlation is defensible at very low revenue because there are few confounders, his illustration being $100 a day of revenue going to $1,000 a day when Facebook is switched on, and it stops being defensible at eight and nine figures where many marketing efforts move the line at once. So the eyeball read has both a floor and a ceiling; above the ceiling the only honest instruments are the geo and holdout designs in the attribution file.

The definition restated on a live unit deck, dated 2025-04-01, which is the earliest instance of it in the file: "what is our blended CAC, this is our total advertising cost divided by new customers acquired, that sits at $58.50, which then takes us down to the final calculation here of profit contribution which is your gross profit minus your CAC." Two things to carry from that sentence. The denominator is new customers acquired, not orders and not conversions. And the CAC is netted against CART-LEVEL gross profit, which is the numerator question settled at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-002|MM-002]] and the cart-versus-unit question at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-157|MM-157]].

Solutions 8 states the stop rule that follows, and pointedly refuses to name a price at which paid media becomes too expensive: "while I cannot tell you a single beautiful happy number that says, this is too much, like $5.25 CPC, that's too much. That doesn't exist. What I can tell you is that you will know it's too much when the economics for your business stop making sense." Independent confirmation of the doctrine from the Google side: the kill threshold lives in the business's own numbers, never in a platform metric or a published benchmark.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04; Blue Sense Digital, Two Years Later: What Still Works (And What Doesn't) ft. Michael Nadalin, 2025-12-27; Blue Sense Digital, Be Careful: TikTok Ads Nearly Bankrupted This Business, 2025-03-24; Blue Sense Digital, Stop Over-Attributing: A Pyramid Framework for True ROI, 2025-06-25; Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01; Solutions 8, How Much Should You Spend on Google Ads? (And When to Stop), 2026-05-19

### MM-068 · Derive the CAC target from new-customer AOV and new-customer gross profit, never from sitewide blended figures
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The common error: sitewide AOV $100 times a blended 70% gross profit justifies a $35 CAC target. Split to new customers and it can invert, because returning customers carry higher AOV and acquisition orders carry more discount. His worked split: new-customer AOV $90 at 50% new-customer GP, which moves the correct CAC target materially and can flip a campaign judged profitable into a loss. Same first-time-versus-returning discipline as [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-005|MM-005]], applied to the target rather than the P&L.

Independent convergence from the Google side, stated as a hard precondition on setting any budget at all. Solutions 8: "before you even try to calculate a single dime of what you think you want to spend with Google Ads, there are four things about your business that you need to know stone cold. Your average order value, your gross margin, your conversion rate, and finally, the return profile you actually need from paid media." The ceiling that follows is the business's allowable first-purchase acquisition cost and nothing else: "If your average order value is, let's say, $120, and your business can only support a $25 acquisition cost on that first purchase, that matters a whole freaking lot more than any random industry benchmark somebody drops into a meeting or that you read in a blog post about Google Ads cost." Worked hypothetical, no account behind the $120 and $25. Two operators from different platforms and different books landing on the same derivation is the part to carry: the CAC target is an output of the business, and a benchmark is never an input.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04; Solutions 8, How Much Should You Spend on Google Ads? (And When to Stop), 2026-05-19
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-069 · Price by waterfalling up from the net profit target, not by multiplying COGS; keystone 2x pricing is what roughly 40% of startups do and it structurally underprices
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Keystone (2x COGS) and IMU (3-5x COGS) both apply an arbitrary multiple to a number that excludes every variable fulfilment cost, and neither references elasticity, competitors, or the real cost structure. Correct sequence, bottom up: net profit target 10%, opex 10%, so a 20% contribution target, then add expected CAC ($30), return allowance 3%, discount allowance 3%, shipping ($11) and COGS ($6), and solve for price (about $70 in his example). Then run competitor research. If the required price is uncompetitive and cannot be positioned as premium, kill the product rather than sell it at a loss. The 40% figure is his estimate of the startup market, ASSERTED. Contrast with [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-018|MM-018]], which sets a dropshipping floor rather than a derivation method.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-070 · Back-solve a contribution margin target from net profit plus opex, then troubleshoot any missed P&L line using only the lines above it
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Want 20% net with opex at 15%, and the contribution margin target is 35%. That converts a bottom-of-P&L goal into a number the media team can be held to weekly. Same in dollars: a $1M net target plus $1M opex means a $2M annual contribution margin target. The diagnostic ladder is directional. Miss EBITDA and the fault is opex, marketing, or cost of delivery. Miss contribution margin and it is marketing or cost of delivery. Miss gross margin and it is cost of delivery or the gross-to-net lines (refunds, discounts, shipping collected). Track every line as a percentage month over month to see which is expanding and which is contracting.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-119 · P&L tranche guardrails for a DTC brand: marketing 20-30% of revenue, G&A 10-20%, COGS around 25%, and past 30% on marketing the business probably fails
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Ezra Firestone's model on $10M of revenue: $3M marketing (30%, the top of the survivable band, with most brands sitting 20-30%), $2M G&A covering team, salary and contractors, and $2.5M COGS at an average 25% against a real range of 20-40%. That leaves $2.5M for insurance, shipping and overhead, and about a 20% net margin if residual overhead stays near $500k. He names shipping to the customer as the bucket where brands quietly lose money. The advertising consequence is the one to carry: the marketing tranche is a hard ceiling on total CAC spend, so a CAC target that requires more than 30% of revenue is not a media-buying problem, it is a business that does not close.

Faris takes the operating expense line materially lower in the current environment: operations at 15% or less rather than 20%, G&A under 10%, and he expects the best brands to reach sub-8%, while noting these are hard to hit below a few million in revenue. Every point taken off G&A is a point available to the marketing tranche or to margin, which is what raises the affordable CAC. He treats the discipline as capital allocation rather than cost-cutting, because getting lean everywhere else is what creates room to fund creative production ([[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-139|MM-139]]). Read the people-cost benchmark in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-057|MM-057]] (5% good, 3% exceptional) as a subset of the G&A line, not a competing number.

Firestone's directional warning on the COGS tranche: across his portfolio the COGS share of the P&L has been rising pretty rapidly year over year for the last couple of years, which he attributes to tariffs, wars and fuel cost, alongside a strained middle class that most brands sell to. He says unit economics is the one thing that has genuinely got harder in 20 years. A rising COGS share compresses the marketing tranche from underneath, so a CAC target set two years ago is probably already unaffordable. All figures asserted from portfolio observation; no P&L or year-over-year numbers were shown.

What it looks like when the guardrails are ignored, from a third source and stated as the common case rather than an outlier. Brands ramp operating expenses in lockstep with revenue, producing accounts at $200,000 a month top line carrying $140,000 a month of operating expense BEFORE ad spend: "200k Top Line a month 140k Opex not inclusive of AD Bend and I'm just looking at it going what is going on here". ASSERTED, no P&L shown, and no COGS figure given, so the contribution left over cannot be computed exactly. The operating point survives the missing lines. At a 70% opex ratio there is almost nothing left to cover media, so the ROAS the account has to hit is set by the org chart rather than by the product, and a media buyer handed that target is being asked to solve a hiring decision. This is the mechanism behind [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-008|MM-008]] stated as a ratio.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Ezra Firestone: Nothing Has Changed In Ecom In 20 Years, 2026-05-15; Andrew Faris, The One Part Of Your Business Where You Should Be Wasting More Money, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, The Perfect Org Structure for eCommerce with Angus Cowan, 2025-03-07
