### MM-157 · Compute e-commerce unit economics at CART level, never per unit, because the average cart carries 3 to 4 units while shipping and discount attach once to the order
Tier: T3 · Status: active
"Within the context of e-commerce you're generally not actually looking at the unit level, you're normally going to look at the cart level, and the reason for that is it just doesn't make sense to look at a unit level when you have three to four units on average within a cart and then there's a shipping cost and a discount rate associated with that cart value." Shipping and discount are levied once per order regardless of unit count, so a per-unit deck either double-counts them across the units or drops them entirely.

The definition chain that follows is the part practitioners get wrong, because the terms are routinely swapped with AOV. Gross retail equals average unit retail times units per transaction, and it is the effective price BEFORE discounts, returns and shipping collected: "gross retail is not average order value, it's not average customer value, it is average unit retail times units per transaction." From gross retail you subtract discounts and returns, add shipping collected, and only then do you have the revenue figure that CAC is netted against. Everything downstream in this file keys off that order, including breakeven ROAS ([[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-010|MM-010]]), the allowable CAC ([[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-068|MM-068]]) and the AOV correction in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-035|MM-035]]. Worked live on a real deck, definitional rather than tested.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-152 · Track new-customer rate (new customers divided by total transactions) at AD level, because a blended CAC swings on the new-versus-returning mix without acquisition changing at all
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Charley T's replacement for what he calls the fake version of CAC. "Instead of just focusing on the fake version of CAC, what I really would love for you to do is begin to look at new customer rate. That is new customer divided by total transactions." The problem it solves is real and common: a CAC computed across all transactions moves whenever the mix moves, so the number changes while acquisition efficiency is flat. Measured at ad level it separates ads that buy customers from ads that harvest people the business already owns.

His kill rule sets the cut line at the account's own repeat share rather than at an absolute threshold: "let's say 60% of your sales are coming from repeat customers and you're struggling to get new people. Turn off every ad where over 60% of those transactions are from repeat customers." Two cautions before running it. New-versus-returning is not an Ads Manager column, so this needs an order-level join back to the store, and without it the metric does not exist. And it is the ad-level twin of the leakage diagnosis in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-071|MM-071]], so the same boundary applies: a declared retargeting ad with a measured incremental lift and a known cost per returning order is outside this test, not failing it. Asserted, no account shown.
Sources: Professor Charley T, If You Run Facebook Ads... ROAS Doesn't Matter in 2026, 2026-01-21
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-154 · A $10M+ agency has never tracked cost per thousand REACHED internally and has never once put it in a client report; it reports spend, revenue, ROAS, CAC and contribution margin
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Andrew Faris, on a book of $10M+ e-commerce clients: "we've almost never had an internal conversation about CPMr. We've basically never talked about that issue. I've never once, literally ever, showed somebody a report that said, here is your CPMr, here is your reach in a different way than before... We've been trying to attack CPMr forever. We just never called it that. What we called it was spend. And what we called it was revenue, and what we called it was ROAS, and what we called it was contribution margin." Five metrics on the report, and a reach or impression cost is not one of them.

The definition first, because the metric is real even if the reporting line is not. CPM prices a thousand impressions; CPMr prices a thousand unique people REACHED. The two diverge as frequency rises, so CPMr can climb while CPM sits flat if delivery keeps returning to the same people. Faris's identity for dismissing it as a standalone: "performance equals spend, which equals reach," bounded by a cost of acquiring a customer above which you stop wanting to spend. If reach is what spend buys, then the outcome metrics already contain the reach story and reporting it separately adds a column without adding a decision.

The harder principle is the one that makes this a reporting rule rather than a preference: cheap reach is purchasable on demand. "If you want to reach the most people with your ads, you should run reach campaigns or link clicks campaigns or video views campaigns because the CPMs on those campaigns on Meta ads is dramatically cheaper than running conversion optimized campaigns. But the reason you don't do that is because you understand that there's a level of shopping intent inherent in purchase optimization that makes the click more valuable to you." Any reach or impression cost figure can therefore be improved instantly by switching campaign objective, which means the figure read alone tells a client nothing about whether their money worked. This directly supports our house client-reporting rule, that reports carry outcome metrics and business math rather than platform-side efficiency columns, and it is the strongest external statement of it in the codex. The audience-side reading of the same quote belongs to the AU file. Practitioner testimony across one agency's book, no report sample or cost data shown.
Sources: Andrew Faris, I've Never Tracked CPMr For My $10M+ Clients. Here's Why., 2026-04-24
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-156 · A blended CAC across a page selling both one-time and subscription hides two very different acquisition costs, and the page itself cannot reveal the split
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Relay of Drew Fallon, with a worked hypothetical. "So many high LTV brands are paying a huge amount of money for subscribers. And the problem is none of them actually know what their subscription CAC is... you might be paying $50 for your one-time purchases and $150 for your subscribers, and it looks like $100 in the end cuz it averages out to the middle... And there's actually no way to disentangle that on the page." The $50, $150 and $100 are invented for the illustration.

The mechanism is worth carrying beyond subscription. Whenever one landing page or one form offers two paths with different economics, the blended cost of acquisition is an average of two numbers you never see, and no reporting cut recovers them because the split happens after the click. Disentangling it takes a split test or removing one option. Direct analogue for lead generation: a blended cost per lead across a form that captures two qualification tiers hides the same thing, and a business that reports one CPL across both is steering on an average it cannot act on. Same family as [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-068|MM-068]] (never set a target off a blended figure) and [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-017|MM-017]] (mean order value hiding the distribution). Reasoning plus invented numbers, no test.
Sources: Andrew Faris, A DEEP Dive On How Retention Works For Ecommerce Businesses, 2026-04-21
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-159 · Unit-economics lever ranking on one real account: units per transaction first, then average unit retail, then discount rate, then COGS and product mix, then CAC
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Every sensitivity below is a MODEL run on the live baseline at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-010|MM-010]] ($16.76 profit contribution, 3.36 units per cart, 22% first-time discount, $58.50 blended CAC). No change was implemented and no outcome was measured, so the arithmetic is deterministic and the business result is unknown. Tier accordingly.

Lever one, units per transaction, which he ranks above price: "the most effective lever to change profit out of average unit retail and units per transaction is units per transaction, makes a substantially bigger impact than average unit retail, and that's why people spend so much time talking about upsells, cross-sells, bundling, rather than just increasing prices." UPT from 3.36 to 4 takes profit contribution from $16.76 to $40, which he self-corrects down to roughly $32 to $35 once COGS follows the extra units. Carry the correction, not the $40. The downside runs the same way and harder: UPT down to 1.7 turns the acquisition order into a $42 LOSS, so a high UPT is what is keeping this account's acquisition solvent at all.

Lever two, average unit retail. A flat $5 rise on every product takes contribution from $16.76 to $28, close to doubling it, because the increase drops straight to the bottom line. Read it against [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-100|MM-100]] before acting: a price rise can leave front-end conversion untouched and still cut repurchase 30 to 40%, which this model does not capture.

Lever three, discount rate, banked at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-086|MM-086]] with the structural-versus-promotional split that has to be made first. Lever four, cost of goods and product mix, and the useful part is that it does not require a supplier renegotiation: rank products by net sales over 12 months, take the top decile, then push creative focus and budget onto the high-gross-margin products inside it. A $5 blended COGS reduction improves this business's profit by 33%. Lever five, marketing efficiency: blended CAC from $58.50 to $48, a $10 move, doubles profit in the model. Return rate is ranked last and mostly dismissed, "one in 40 that I look at, return rates are an opportunity, but most it isn't." Shipping collected has its own benchmark at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-025|MM-025]].

The levers compound, which is the actual thesis: 5 to 10% on each line at once (price $46 to $48, UPT 3.36 to 3.45, discount 22% to 17-18%, an extra $0.50 of shipping collected, CAC down 5% to $53) takes profit contribution from $16.76 to $34, doubling it with no single large change. Nick Theriot models the identical structure independently on a different account, taking cost per link click, AOV and conversion rate each up 10% ($2.20 to $1.98, $97.63 to $107.39, 4.11% to 4.52%) and reporting that profit roughly doubles on the same spend. Two operators, different lever sets, same compounding claim, both modelled rather than executed.

The caution is load-bearing and must travel with the ranking: this ordering is an ARTEFACT of an account already sitting at 3.36 units per transaction. A single-item-cart business has almost no UPT lever and would rank price or CAC first. A thin-margin business would rank COGS first. Never present the ranking as universal. What transfers is the method, which is to build the deck, move one line at a time, and rank the levers for YOUR account before choosing what to work on.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01; Nick Theriot, Best Meta Ads Columns Setup for Ecommerce (2026), 2026-07-03
Last touched: 2026-08-20

## Margin, ROAS Targets, and Bid Power

### MM-010 · Gross margin sets the ROAS breakeven: 87 points landed margin breaks even at 1.3 ROAS first-purchase; 50-60 points needs roughly 2.0
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A client product with 87 points of landed margin is profitable on first purchase at 1.3 ROAS, meaning ads barely need to perform to make money; another brand sits at 75+ points. At a typical 50-60 points of landed margin you live in the world of needing roughly a 2:1 ROAS constantly. High margin creates a strong incentive to deploy maximum spend and creative volume fast because the downside of a losing test is small.

The formulas, stated twice by Blue Sense Digital across two videos: breakeven ROAS = 1 / gross margin %, and breakeven CAC = gross margin in dollars. Worked example: $100 AOV with $60 cost of delivery gives 40% margin, breakeven ROAS 2.5, breakeven CAC $40. Now discount 30%. Price falls to $70, COGS stays $60, margin becomes $10, breakeven CAC becomes $10 and breakeven ROAS becomes **7.0**, a 2.8x efficiency requirement bought with a single 30% discount, which is a number almost no paid account clears on cold traffic. **CORRECTED 2026-08-30: this entry previously read "breakeven ROAS becomes 10" and "a 5x efficiency requirement". Both were wrong. 70 divided by 10 is 7.0, and 7.0 over 2.5 is 2.8x. The same operator states 7 out loud in a later video and puts the multiple at "a 3x in the return on ad spend that you're currently achieving", so the source was right and this file was wrong.** The basket-lift qualifier that goes with it is at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-193|MM-193]]. Run the calculation for every product and again at every discount level before the offer goes live, and ask whether the account has ever hit the resulting number. Every media buyer and agency on the account should know the breakeven figure per product. Deterministic arithmetic applied to real decisions, ASSERTED with illustrative numbers rather than exported account data. Restated a third time on the Google side at collection level: a 50% gross margin collection breaks even at 2.0 ROAS and a 65% collection at 1.6, which is a small-sounding margin gap and a large efficiency gap. The structural consequence of that pair is banked at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-017|MM-017]].

A live account puts real numbers on the margin-to-contribution chain, banked 2026-08-20. One month, roughly 2,062 first-time orders against 836 returning, 3.36 units per cart, a 22% first-time discount rate. "That takes us to a gross margin of 56.77%... what is our blended CAC... that sits at $58.5 which then takes us down to the final calculation here of profit contribution which is your gross profit minus your CAC which comes out to $16.76 which sits at a CM3 percentage or contribution margin percentage of 12.61%." So 56.77 points of gross margin at a $58.50 blended CAC leaves 12.61 points of contribution. Figures stated during a walkthrough of a real account, not exported and not baselined against a prior period, so T3.

The rule he attaches converts that into the ceiling on the whole business: when returning customers drive under about 15% of monthly revenue, contribution margin percentage IS the upper bound on net profit, because there is no second revenue stream to add margin behind it. At 12.61% contribution and a realistically lean 10% operating expense load, "your current maximum net profit is 2%". That is the number that forces the decision, since 2% net is not a business worth running and the only two exits are an acquisition fix (lower CAC or higher gross margin) or a retention fix (get returning revenue past 15%). Read it as the diagnostic form of [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-070|MM-070]], which back-solves the contribution target from the net profit goal; this runs the same arithmetic forwards from the account you actually have.
Sources: Andrew Faris, You're Working Hard On The Wrong Problem. Here's How To Tell., 2026-07-27; Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04; Blue Sense Digital, Best Google Ad Account Structure for eCommerce in 2025, 2025-09-02; Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01; Blue Sense Digital, How To Increase Margin While Discounting This Black Friday, 2025-08-20
Last touched: 2026-08-30

### MM-011 · Improving gross margin via supply chain beats improving ads: one brand moved manufacturing countries and went from ~50 to 65-70 points landed
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A brand at roughly 50 points of landed margin net of returns relocated manufacturing to another country and projects 65-70 points, dramatically raising how fast it can grow without any ad improvement. The rule: if gross margin is the binding lever, chasing supply chain (new suppliers, renegotiation) improves outcomes without making the ads a single moment better, and should shape which products get advertising firepower.
Sources: Andrew Faris, You're Working Hard On The Wrong Problem. Here's How To Tell., 2026-07-27
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-012 · Returning-customer revenue raises the tolerable CPA and lets you outbid single-product competitors; every $100k+/mo spender has retention products, added around $30k/mo spend
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Higher LTV supports a higher front-end CPA, described as the ability to "bully out all the competitors and spend a lot more"; single-product, no-retention businesses hit a bid ceiling that LTV businesses do not. Every client spending $100k+/month on Facebook has multiple retention products, and they added them at roughly $30k/month spend specifically to raise LTV so a higher CAC ceiling became affordable. Blue Sense makes the same structural point: your sustainable bid IS your unit economics, and the competitor with better margin, AOV, and LTV wins more auctions with worse creative. Ben Heath adds a fourth independent statement of the same law plus the lever-ranking that follows from it: amateurs work on cutting cost per purchase from $26 to $23, professionals work on raising customer value from $120 to $320, because the second lever has far more range and therefore moves profit more. His illustration is Starbucks at roughly $7 per visit but over $14,000 per customer over a lifetime, which is what lets a business like that pay $200-500 to acquire someone; his generalization is that there are many large businesses with low single-transaction value and none with low lifetime value. Named LTV levers: subscriptions, upsells, significantly more expensive upsells, referral programs, and follow-up sequences (paid or owned) to drive the repeat purchase. He frames acquisition as an arms race where the operator who can afford to pay most per customer wins over the long run, matching MM-042.

Professor Charley T states the negative case twice and names the escapes: if a customer buys once and never again you have a product, not a business, so every scaling decision hangs on first-purchase margin alone and the account hits its ceiling immediately. The three fixes he lists are a self-liquidating offer that drives the front-end CAC negative, a bundle or genuine second product, or referral-driven repeat business, and he counts referral as the second transaction in models like real estate where the same buyer never returns. Asserted, no data shown, but it converts the bid-ceiling observation into a product-roadmap requirement.

Theriot states the same law twice more with the stage attached. At $500k to $1m a month the binding problem is that CAC rises structurally with spend, and his named counter is raising LTV so the higher CAC stays affordable rather than defending the old CAC. At scale the constraint stops being creative and becomes LTV outright: "You can even break even on the ad side but still build a profitable business through retention revenue... you could spend four times more to acquire a customer than the competitor ever could," including against a competitor with the better product. Narrative, no data.

Ben Heath supplies the behavioural half, which is why operators sit below their own ceiling. Small advertisers systematically underprice acquisition against their own stated willingness to pay: an advertiser with a $2,000 customer will cap at $100 to $200, then agree immediately when the same question is framed mechanically, "you're telling me that you wouldn't be willing if you had a magic machine and you could put $400 in it or $500 in it and it just spits out a $2,000 customer, you wouldn't do it." The downstream error is the one to look for in a real account: a $20 cost-per-lead cap at a 1-in-10 lead-to-customer rate, which prices customers at $200 and stops the account scaling at the ad set level long before the business economics bind. Worked hypothetical, no account attached.
Fifth operator, dating the ceiling rather than describing it. "It used to be the case where you could be profitable just selling one product at like, you know, 2 to 3x rorows... It's 2026 now, and that's not the case anymore." No CPM series, no margin math and no cohort behind the degradation claim, so the year attached to it is his impression rather than a measured break point. It is worth logging only because it is a fourth independent voice landing on the same structural conclusion from a dropshipping vantage rather than a retention one, and because the direction it names (the same front-end ROAS buying less profit than it used to) is testable against a brand's own history.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I spend $100k a day on Facebook Ads (here's who will scale & fail in 2027), 2026-08-14; Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads (here's what's scaling in 2026), 2026-08-03; Blue Sense Digital, How The Meta Ads Algorithm Works in 2026, 2026-07-28; Ben Heath, Learn 97% of Meta Ads in Under 29 Minutes, 2026-08-18; Professor Charley T, If you run Facebook Ads... everything just changed., 2025-12-06; Nick Theriot, The 7 Levels of Scaling Facebook Ads, 2026-06-24; Nick Theriot, Every Level of Scaling an ECOM Brand ($0, $30k, $100k, $1m, $10m), 2026-06-10; Ben Heath, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026, 2026-02-20; Mark Builds Brands, how to turn any product into a money printer w branded dropshipping, 2026-02-17
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-013 · In-platform ROAS understates subscription and repeat-purchase brands, so winner/loser calls need metrics beyond ROAS
Tier: T3 · Status: active
ROAS "is just a number" that ignores subscription revenue and downstream repeat purchases. For subscription brands an ad's true value exceeds its attributed ROAS, so check other metrics (CPA, CTR, funnel position) before killing or crowning an ad.

Ben Heath states the same thing with a number and a working threshold, reading a live account: "purchase ROAS of 1.62 doesn't sound great, but this business is very much one that generates tons of repeat purchases. So the eventual customer value ends up being far, far larger than that initial transaction, which is the only thing that's accounted for in this return on ad spend number." He treats anything above 1.0 as acceptable on a genuine repeat-purchase business and 1.62 as good. The precondition is the one [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-003|MM-003]] fights over: "generates tons of repeat purchases" has to be a measured cohort figure, not a belief, or this is just a low ROAS with a story attached.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, How to Make Meta Ads So Good People Can't Stop Watching, 2026-08-16; Ben Heath, How Good Is Meta's New AI Business Assistant?, 2026-06-03
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-014 · Optimize profit dollars leaving the business, not the ROAS ratio: doubling volume at 2x can beat holding at 4x
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The gap between a scaling business and a dying one is often only 10-20% on ROAS or CPA. Volume unlocks supplier price negotiation, better shipping rates, better talent, and an unmeasurable word-of-mouth engine. There is a button to spend more money but no button that guarantees a ROAS, which is why no famous brand runs a 50-100x ROAS but hundreds spend $1k-$1M/day.

Restated 2026-08-17 with a worked comparison and the compounding argument that carries it. The gap that decides profit or loss is tiny: a brand targeting 2.2 ROAS and hitting 2.0 loses money on every customer, and at 2.2 makes money on every customer, so most brands need a 10-30% ROAS improvement, not a doubling. The failure mode is chasing the ratio: brands that hyper-optimize ROAS reach 5x, 6x, 7x and then cannot spend past about $100/day, because scale falls faster than the ratio rises. His illustrative comparison runs a 2.2 ROAS spending far more against a 7.0 ROAS spending a fraction: the 2.2 case drives roughly 3x the revenue, costs much more to fulfil, and nets about $430 profit, close to what the 7.0 case nets on much less spend. The tiebreaker is customer count, since roughly 3x revenue means roughly 3x new customers, so any lifetime value at or above about 1.1x compounds the low-ROAS case into a much larger business over time. Numbers are illustrative slides, not account exports. This makes MM-014 dependent on real measured repeat purchase, so it should be read against the contested MM-003 and the cohort discipline in MM-006 before acting on it.

Three additions from Professor Charley T across two videos. First, a second worked example running through AOV instead of spend volume: a 5x ROAS on $10 spend at a $50 AOV yields $40 gross profit, while a 2x ROAS on a $100 CPA at a $200 AOV yields $100, a 2.5x profit gap in favour of the worse ratio. He ties this to product mix, arguing a ROAS-managed account never learns to sell the higher-ticket SKU because the ratio always favours the cheap one. Second, the ratio does not even rank ads correctly inside one account: he reports four ads all at 1.6 ROAS carrying profit per sale from $20 to $100, and cites pairs at 1.2 ROAS/$60 and 1.9 ROAS/$100, with no correlation between the two columns. Third, a case: a fashion brand's ROAS improved from 1.2 to 3.0 after hiring an agency while revenue, profit and spend all collapsed, because the agency optimized toward easy-to-sell, low-repeat products and toward people who had already bought. Same brand reportedly went 8x in eight weeks after the approach was reversed. He says the ad-account screenshots exist in a separate case-study video; nothing was shown on stream, so this stays T3.

Nick Theriot states the operating consequence as a metric choice: he manages accounts on CPA rather than ROAS, because a CPA can be modelled against AOV, LTV and margin in a spreadsheet while a ROAS carries no business meaning on its own. His worked line is $24 to acquire a customer against a $60 first-purchase AOV and a $150 LTV over 12 months at 40% profit, from which the affordable spend follows directly. Illustrative numbers, no account attached.

Restated four more times across the same speaker's 2025-12 to 2026-02 run, with the position hardened. His strongest form: ROAS and profit have no correlation at all, illustrated by a 3x ROAS at zero profit against a 0.8 ROAS at $100,000 profit in a week. He then concedes a higher ROAS is more likely to be profitable than a lower one before repeating that the correlation does not hold in general, which is the honest reading to carry. Another worked pair on the same arithmetic: a $2 product returning $10 is a 5 ROAS banking $8, a $50 product returning $100 is a 2 ROAS banking $50, six times more cash on the worse ratio. Carry the counterweight he supplies himself, because it cuts against reading this claim as "spend more": he says he once added $12M a year in EBITDA to a business by cutting $30,000 a day in ad spend, with no account, vertical, prior ROAS or revenue impact given. The doctrine is profit dollars, and on some accounts that means less spend, not more.

Third independent operator on the same doctrine, banked 2026-08-20. Ben Heath: "Much better to generate a 4x return on ad spend spending a million dollars a month than a 10x return on ad spend spending $10,000 a month. You make a lot more money at the lower return on ad spend number because you've achieved scale." He reports his own case-study spread as 20x on some accounts and 3.2x at scale on others, and counts both as successes, which is the same refusal to rank accounts by ratio. Charley T restates the price-point version in a fourth video, "a 5x on a $10 CPA is less than half of the profit of a 2x on a $100 CPA. That's a $40 GPT versus a $100 GPT," so the pair is now stated across at least five sessions and three operators. Theriot's reason for managing on CPA is the cleanest statement of why the ratio cannot be the KPI: a CPA models against LTV in a spreadsheet and a ROAS does not. "How much you spend to acquire a customer? Three ex return on ad spend. It's like, well, that doesn't make any sense." His worked target that session was a $65 CPA against a $200 three-month LTV.
Sources: Sam Piliero, Easiest Way to Scale Facebook Ads in 2026, 2026-08-03; Sam Piliero, Why total profit beats profit per order with Meta ads, 2026-08-17; Professor Charley T, The BEST Facebook Ads Strategy for 2026 Post Andromeda, 2025-12-27; Professor Charley T, The NEW SIMPLE EASY WAY to Scale BIG with Meta Ads, 2026-02-07; Professor Charley T, Mastering Andromeda: Creative Testing and Scaling, 2025-12-13; Professor Charley T, Q5: the 2nd Black Friday: Meta Ads for Profit, 2025-12-20; Professor Charley T, How to Scale Profits with Meta Ads, 2026-01-24; Professor Charley T, How to Scale Facebook Ads in 2026, 2026-02-28; Nick Theriot, Sh*t I care about when scaling Facebook Ads, 2026-07-10; Professor Charley T, If You Run Facebook Ads... ROAS Doesn't Matter in 2026, 2026-01-21; Ben Heath, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026, 2026-02-20; Nick Theriot, Best Meta Ads Columns Setup for Ecommerce (2026), 2026-07-03
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-015 · Run the account against ONE "king goal" metric; every other metric is only a proxy solved backward from it
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Businesses with 20 targets (CPC, CTR, conversion rate, ad-launch quotas) scale worst; the most effective scaling brands set one precise target (e.g. 8.0 ROAS) and derive what cost-per-add-to-cart, cost-per-initiated-checkout, and conversion rate must be to hit it via custom columns. Example managed brand: $43,000 spend in 30 days at 8.4 ROAS against an 8.0 king goal.

Second operator, with the account on screen, and it turns the king goal into a scaling gate rather than only a reporting target. Theriot's single metric is the client's own cost per purchase threshold, stated as $50 or better, against $35 actual on the day of the walkthrough. The headroom between actual and threshold is what licenses the next budget increase, and the day the threshold is missed the budget holds flat while a new creative is found rather than being cut. Both figures read off the live account. The step size itself is a scaling question and lives in the SC file; what belongs here is that the gate is a unit-economics number handed over by the client, not a platform metric.
Third operator names the metric rather than leaving the king goal abstract: customer acquisition cost is the only North Star, and every other rate in the funnel is solved backward from it. He attaches a constraint on where the target comes from. Benchmarks must be pulled from your own vertical rather than from what other operators quote, because a cost per booked call or a close rate carried across verticals prices a different business. ASSERTED, no data. It is the same discipline as the king goal, with the added rule that the goal's VALUE is not borrowable even when the method is.
Sources: Sam Piliero, Easiest Way to Scale Facebook Ads in 2026, 2026-08-03; Nick Theriot, How To Test Facebook Ads in 2026 When You Can't Find a Winner, 2026-08-19; Dr. Matt Shiver, Facebook Ads Tutorial for Beginners Updated for 2026, 2026-03-24
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-016 · Realistic front-end ROAS on high-ticket lead gen is 2-5x; a forecast whose worst case does not lose money is wrong
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Model best/average/worst for CPL, cost per call, show rate, and close rate (example worst case: $30 CPL, $400 cost per call, 50% show, 10% close). Best-case-on-everything (10x ROAS in his example) never happens because metrics trade off against each other; a healthy scalable range is $2-5 back per $1 in on the front end. If the modeled worst case still profits, the inputs are dishonest.

The same operator runs the model backwards to derive a ceiling on cost per booked call, which is the form worth carrying into forecast work. "if we know that our target realizes at least a 3x on the front end, And we know our show rate's 50% close rate's 20%, that means we need 10 calls to book one deal. And if at a 3x ROAS target, we would need to spend $300 max for a booked call". The arithmetic is sound: 50% show times 20% close is 10 calls per deal, and a 3x front-end target caps the booked call at $300. Two limits on using it. It is arithmetic on assumed rates rather than an observed account, so it is a MODEL and never evidence. And a $300 ceiling at 10 calls per deal and a 3x target implies roughly a $9,000 offer, which he never states, so the number is unusable until the offer price is filled in with your own. His stated close rates are also 20% here and 40% in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-160|MM-160]], a factor of two on the same operator's own funnel, which doubles or halves this ceiling on its own.

One real cost per booked call to sit next to the modelled one, from a different session: $200 per booked call on a $20,000 to $30,000 done-for-you offer in an uncrowded niche. Recalled on camera, no spend level and no volume attached. At those figures the ceiling would be far above $300, which is the point of deriving it from your own offer rather than borrowing his.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Run Facebook Ads for Coaches & Agency Owners (FREE COURSE), 2026-08-04; Dr. Matt Shiver, Facebook Ads Tutorial for Beginners Updated for 2026, 2026-03-24; Dr. Matt Shiver, Two 7-Figure Coaches Compare Their Skool Funnels, 2026-06-11
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-017 · Setting a CPA target as sitewide AOV divided by target ROAS is wrong for wide-catalog brands: mean, median, and modal order values diverge wildly
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Example brand: mean AOV $1,027, median $512, modal order value $141. A single blended CPA target derived from mean AOV systematically overspends on low-AOV products and underspends on high-AOV products, misdistributing the budget. Fix: group products into economic units with similar AOV/margin/LTV (e.g. ad sets in a CBO each representing one unit) or use min ROAS bidding, which bakes AOV into the control.

Faris caveats his own framework: AOV is a stand-in used because the arithmetic is easier, and the real grouping variables are contribution margin, return on invested capital and LTV. Two products with different AOVs that support the same CAC because their margins differ belong together in one ad set. Two products with the same AOV and different margins do not. Where margin profiles diverge across the catalogue, the AOV coherence rule stops applying and the grouping has to be done on margin directly. He frames the whole exercise as capital allocation rather than account structure.

Independent second operator, arriving from Google account structure rather than Meta ad sets, and stating the rule as a gate on segmentation: segment campaigns only where unit economics differ. His worked case is a furniture brand stocking third-party ranges at 20 to 30% gross margin alongside its own manufactured range at 70%, where gross profit per sale is 2.5x apart, so the two ranges need separate campaigns and separate efficiency targets and the gain outweighs the fragmentation cost. At collection level the same split is 50% margin breaking even at 2.0 ROAS against 65% at 1.6. The decision rule attached is the part to carry, because it names what consolidation costs: a blended structure can report a HIGHER ROAS while producing fewer profit dollars than a segmented one carrying per-margin targets. ROAS is therefore the wrong objective function for the structure decision, which is [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-014|MM-014]] stated at the campaign layer. Worked margins, no profit-dollar comparison shown.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Do Cost Caps Work? Taylor Holiday Brings $200M Of Data, 2026-08-06; Andrew Faris, How To Fix The Most Common Bid Cap & Cost Cap Mistake I See, 2026-06-25; Blue Sense Digital, 6 Step Checklist to Increase Google Ad Performance, 2025-03-06; Blue Sense Digital, Best Google Ad Account Structure for eCommerce in 2025, 2025-09-02
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-018 · Dropshipping margin floor: at least $25 gross margin per unit AND a selling price of at least 3x COGS
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Both rules must hold: e.g. $10 landed cost sold at $50 gives $40 margin (above $25) and 5x markup (above 3x). Products set up with breakeven ROAS around 1.7-2.0 require sustaining roughly a 3x ROAS at scale, which "doesn't happen" at high spend levels. Margins are one of the few controllable inputs, unlike the CPA Facebook gives you.

CONTESTED on the role of the first product. Professor Charley T rejects single-product hunting entirely and says dropshipping is about building an email list at break-even: the front product's only job is to be the best possible lead magnet into a second purchase, so acquire it at break-even and recover the margin through the list at zero incremental media cost. That directly opposes the first-order margin floor above, because a break-even front end by definition fails the $25-gross-margin test. Mark Builds Brands is T3 with a case study behind the floor; Charley T's version is asserted with no data. What resolves it is a measured repeat rate and repeat window on a dropshipped catalogue: the break-even strategy is only solvent where a real second purchase exists, which is the same condition [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-003|MM-003]] turns on. Absent that measurement, the margin floor is the safe rule.
**Two problems with the numbers in this claim, both found 2026-09-05, and neither changes the rule's direction.**

**One, the "3x" is written in the keystone convention and the body mislabels it.** The heading says selling price of at least 3x cost of goods, which is unambiguous. The worked example then calls $10 landed cost sold at $50 a "5x markup". Five is the price-to-cost ratio; by the actual markup formula, (price less cost) over cost, that product is 4x. **Anyone applying the formal definition to the "3x" would impose a floor of price = 4x cost, which is a materially stricter rule than intended.** Read every multiple in this claim as price divided by cost. The two competing definitions and the conversion formulas are at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-210|MM-210]].

**Two, the operator behind the floor states it at two different numbers three months apart.** On 2026-04-24 he gives it as at least **$30** of gross margin per unit and 3x cost of goods, with the same shoe-box and passionate-market conditions attached. On 2026-07-30, the source already on this claim, it is **$25**. Same speaker, same rule, same structure, different absolute threshold, and he never acknowledges the change. Treat the floor as "roughly $25 to $30 of gross margin per unit" rather than as a precise number, because it is a rule of thumb that moves.

Sources: Mark Builds Brands, f*ck it. ZERO to $1k/day in 3 days with ai dropshipping. [full case study], 2026-07-30; Professor Charley T, If you run Facebook Ads... everything just changed., 2025-12-06; Mark Builds Brands, how to print money with AI (before NPCs ruin it), 2026-04-24
Last touched: 2026-09-05

### MM-079 · Pick the SKU you advertise by second-purchase rate, days to second purchase, and combined first-plus-second AOV, not by first-purchase volume
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Data SHOWN twice. A real supplement client's Shopify order export was read live on screen: the SKU with a 53% second-purchase rate at roughly 40 days was getting no ad support, while the heavily advertised SKU sat under 30% at roughly 50 days. His rule on that comparison is blunt: a SKU that takes 12 more days to produce $40 less is a liability regardless of unit volume. A second dashboard, sorted by number of first purchases, showed shilajit capsules at 47 days and $57 combined AOV losing to collagen peptides at 44 days and $98 combined AOV, meaning the top-first-purchase product was the wrong thing to advertise. A third case, asserted only: a $98 supplement SKU repurchasing at 57% beat the $50 SKUs repurchasing at 30%, and scaling only that product moved gross profit per transaction from $17 to $37 in four days (four days is far too short to call that durable).

The no-tooling version: pull six months of receipts, filter to customers who came back, and find the single most common item they bought as new customers. That is the acquisition product. The catalog then splits into an acquisition catalog and a retention catalog, and the pixel should be trained on the hero SKU via a dedicated custom conversion. Price sensitivity gets worked on top: at a $20 acquisition cost, two sales at $40 with a $67 combined AOV leave about $17 profit, while $40 a unit returns the money three days faster and is worth about $18. Caveat: one of the dashboards is his own paid product, so treat the tool as a vendor demo and the metric set as the transferable part. This is the computable version of [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-042|MM-042]]'s qualitative criteria and the selection procedure underneath [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-012|MM-012]] and [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-029|MM-029]].

Four more live account reads, three of them on screen, banked 2026-08-19. A 10,666-order women's apparel store: the Renaissance handbag had 50 first purchases and 25 second, a 50% repeat rate at 110 days, beating SKUs with 66 and 68 first purchases that repeated less; the Velvet Dreams Cammy hit 53% at 77 days; a tasselled floral pin repeated for a third of buyers inside 45 days. A second pass on the same store (about 11,000 orders, roughly $2M trailing revenue) found a product with half the top seller's first purchases but 70% of its second purchases, 53% of them repurchasing under 60 days, and two similarly priced blazers $40 apart on combined AOV and 11 days apart on repeat ($299 at 71 days versus $335 at 42 days). A supplement brand's cohort dashboard is the cleanest demonstration that ROAS does not select the hero SKU: magnesium ran roughly a 2 ROAS and the owner was pushing it, but only 29% of those buyers ever came back, while over half of peptide buyers did. Split by first-purchase product, magnesium showed $39,000 new against $3,000 returning and the peptide showed $39,000 against $39,000.

Prevalence check that cuts against the claim, banked 2026-08-19. Blue Sense reports that entry-point lifetime value varies enough to change acquisition aggression in only about 10% of the brands they onboard, and that for everyone else lifetime value after the first purchase does not fluctuate much by entry product. Status stays active rather than contested, because MM-079 rests on repeated live account reads with the figures on screen while the 10% is a recalled frequency with nothing shown, so the tier gap runs the wrong way for a contest. Note also that the two sides may not be measuring the same quantity: Charley T sorts on second-purchase RATE and time to second purchase, and the 10% claim is about lifetime VALUE among people who did come back. Honest operating form: run the analysis on your own catalogue because it is cheap, and expect it to change the plan on a minority of accounts rather than all of them.

Three rules that fall out. Advertise the two or three SKUs that produce repeat buyers, never the catalogue. Lift an account-wide 20% repurchase rate by promoting only the products that repurchase at 30-40%. Where the second product bought is the same as the first, sell a three-pack and take AOV up about 2.5x immediately. The compact selection formula he states: the biggest gap between AOV and CPA, the shortest time to second purchase, and the most repeat buyers. Outcome case, asserted: Under Outfit went from $50k/month to about $1M/week on the comfort shaping bra with the adjustable strap, picked for highest second-purchase rate, shortest repeat timeline and a workable buy-three-get-one, with LTV moving from about $105 to about $1,000. Behaviour split by age, younger buyers took the single unit then the upsell immediately while older buyers took the bundle up front, which stretched their repeat timeline from roughly 20-odd days to about 60 because they were holding more product. Flag on that case: he elsewhere credits the same Under Outfit result to the rebuttal-upsell mechanism in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-131|MM-131]], so the selection method is well evidenced but the causal attribution of that specific outcome is not.

Under Outfit detail added 2026-08-20, and it shows the offer being derived from the repeat DATA rather than chosen: the hero was one SKU in one colour, the comfort shaping bra in sand, the average repeat interval was about 25 days, and the average customer bought 2.5 times. "As soon as we understood that the average woman then bought two and a half times, we launched a buy three get one free offer to overcome the objection on that." So the bundle depth was set at the observed purchase count, which is the reproducible step. Note the LTV outcome is told differently across sessions, "from like 105 to well over 500 and a thousand" here against about $1,000 elsewhere, so treat the direction as the finding and the multiple as approximate.

Andrew Faris supplies the same diagnosis from real client numbers rather than a dashboard, and it is the version that names the ACTION: "product A, 50% increase in customer value over the course of a year. Product B, it was 75%. Dramatically better retention rate on product B versus product A, and we were acquiring those customers at the same CAC. That's a problem." Equal CAC across entry products with unequal downstream value is a misallocation, and product B probably warrants a higher CAC allowance. His own caveat is the one to hold: the higher-value product may carry less available volume, so the allowance rises but the spend may not. No method or sample size given. This also cuts against the 10% prevalence check above, since a second operator found entry-product value variation worth acting on in a live account.
Sources: Professor Charley T, The BEST Facebook Ads Strategy for 2026 Post Andromeda, 2025-12-27; Professor Charley T, Record Profits: the Meta Ads Andromeda Playbook, 2026-01-03; Professor Charley T, The BEST AD ON META after Andromeda, 2026-01-10; Professor Charley T, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Low Budget, 2026-02-28; Professor Charley T, The NEW SIMPLE EASY WAY to Scale BIG with Meta Ads, 2026-02-07; Professor Charley T, Mastering Andromeda: Creative Testing and Scaling, 2025-12-13; Professor Charley T, If you run Facebook Ads... everything just changed., 2025-12-06; Professor Charley T, Do these 5 things each day to scale Facebook Ads, 2026-02-21; Professor Charley T, Copy this Meta Ad strategy to BLOW UP your business. (After Andromeda, Clothing Niche), 2026-01-31; Blue Sense Digital, Best Google Ad Account Structure for eCommerce in 2025, 2025-09-02; Professor Charley T, Copy This Facebook Ads Strategy (Post-Andromeda), 2026-02-07; Andrew Faris, A DEEP Dive On How Retention Works For Ecommerce Businesses, 2026-04-21

### MM-080 · Build GPT (gross profit per transaction) as a custom Ads Manager metric, then plot every ad on a GPT-versus-CPA grid using the account's own averages as axes
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Build shown in the UI: Columns, Customize columns, Custom, Create custom metric, formula "average purchases conversion value minus cost per purchase", name it GPT, currency format. It puts per-transaction profit in dollars directly on the reporting row, which is the implementation of [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-014|MM-014]]'s profit-dollars doctrine. The 2x2 uses the account's own averages, not external benchmarks. Upper right (better than average on both) are scalers. Lower left are liabilities. Good GPT with bad CPA are optimizers to refine. Good CPA with bad GPT are fake wins, because "it's a lot easier to lower the CPA than to improve the AOV" and those ads pull in the wrong buyer. Boundary case he flags explicitly: an ad with negative GPT but below-average CPA is not a double red flag and does not get cut. Order of operations: scale the scalers, refine the optimizers, cut the liabilities. Caveat he does not state: average purchase value minus CPA is gross of COGS, so it is contribution before product cost unless the value column is already margin-adjusted, which matters below roughly 60 points of landed margin ([[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-010|MM-010]]).

The decision rule GPT replaces, stated on a live viewer question: CPA up 28%, AOV up 15%, GPT up 20%, and his answer is that the CPA number is irrelevant and the correct response is to spend more, because 20% more money per sale is the thing being scaled. He repeats the framing in the same session with a $66 CPA sitting against a $94 GPT. Operating form: a rising CPA is only a problem when GPT falls with it, so read the two columns together and never act on CPA alone. The viewer's numbers are self-reported.

The reason the quadrants prescribe different actions, stated 2026-02-14 and worth banking because it is the part operators get backwards: "these are higher than average CPAs, but also higher than average GPTs. Nothing you really do in the account will get that ad to improve its AOV. If you make an improvement, it's likely to improve every AOV. But you can work on reducing the CPA of an ad. The CPA is the constraint." GPT is a property of the buyer the ad attracts and is not adjustable at the ad level, so on an optimizer there is exactly one lever and it is CPA. That is also why fake wins cannot be rescued by better media buying.

Worked liability off the shown grid: "this one spent 1,100 bucks, got three purchases. CPA is 370. We lose 146 on every sale." A $370 CPA against a GPT that puts the account $146 in the hole per sale, which is a kill without further analysis.

Lead-gen port, from the same session: swap purchase conversion value for lead value or projected lead value and the axes, quadrants and actions all hold. "In lead gen it's the same thing because you're mapping the quality or the value of a lead... change the column header from leads to purchases and then purchase conversion value to lead conversion value or projected lead conversion value." That makes this the account-level implementation of the lead-value bucketing in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-082|MM-082]], and it inherits MM-082's precondition: without offline conversion data flowing back, the lead-value axis is a guess.
Sources: Professor Charley T, The NEW BEST Meta Ads Andromeda Course to Scale in 2026, 2026-01-24; Professor Charley T, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Low Budget, 2026-02-28; Professor Charley T, Do these 5 things each day to scale Facebook Ads, 2026-02-21; Professor Charley T, ROAS is STUPID, Let's Make Money with Meta, 2026-02-14

### MM-081 · Customer uniformity, not ad quality, is what lets an account scale at a worse front-end ROAS
Tier: T4 · Status: active
The causal chain he argues: creative concentration produces a homogeneous acquired cohort, a homogeneous cohort monetizes better downstream (upsell take, second purchase, email and search value), and better downstream monetization lowers the front-end return the account has to hit. He puts the range at needing a 3x versus being able to scale at what the transcript renders as "a 08", almost certainly 0.8. This is the economic argument underneath the few-ads doctrine, and it reaches the same place as [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-012|MM-012]] by a different route: sameness of the cohort rather than the existence of retention products. Entirely asserted, no data. Testing it needs cohort-level AOV and repeat data split by acquisition concept.
Sources: Professor Charley T, The BEST AD ON META after Andromeda, 2026-01-10

### MM-082 · Grade lead-gen ads on downstream close rate, not on cost per lead: ads with identical cost per conversation can close at 80%, 50% and 0%
Tier: T4 · Status: active
The qualitative layer is invisible in Ads Manager, which is the entire reason to build an offline-conversion pipe back into Meta. Once close data returns, the efficiency move is cutting ads that generate conversations nobody closes rather than ads with the highest CPL. Lead-value math attached: on a $10,000 program, a 1-in-20 close rate makes a lead worth $50 and costs 20 sales calls, while 1-in-5 makes the same lead worth $2,000 for five calls. Instruction: bucket leads at $50 / $500 / $1,000 / $2,000 and turn off ads that never produce the top bucket. Numbers illustrative, no test shown. The same speaker's compact form of the argument: form fill to cash is five steps (fill the form, get them to care, move them through the journey, sales call, close), so a cost per lead is a price attached to step one and carries no information about step five. Model, with no rate attached to any of the five steps.
Sources: Professor Charley T, Record Profits: the Meta Ads Andromeda Playbook, 2026-01-03; Professor Charley T, Copy this Facebook Ads Lead Gen strategy, it'll blow up your business, 2026-03-18

### MM-083 · Resellers run about 30% gross margin after delivery and fees, forcing roughly a 10x ROI, which only works when the brands they stock are themselves advertising heavily
Tier: T3 · Status: active
At 30% gross margin after cost of delivery and transaction fees, the reseller has to operate at roughly a 10% MER. That is not a media-buying problem, it is a buying-team dependency. Stock a product whose brand is funding demand and a dynamic carousel can open at 15-20 ROAS. Stock a fading fad or a brand that does not advertise and no media buying rescues the margin structure. ASSERTED from experience, no account data shown. Pairs with [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-046|MM-046]] on TAM compressing margin, and with the observation that high-margin DTC categories buy their margin back in higher CAC.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How To Structure Your Meta Ads for Profit (Free Live Webinar), 2025-02-19
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-084 · Rising CPM and frequency compound over 2-3 years and silently absorb genuine AOV, LTV and conversion-rate gains, so the brand stands still
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Brands do the right work (new product development to raise AOV, retention work to raise LTV, CRO to raise conversion rate) and get no compounding benefit, because ad cost inflation takes it. Kiel's read on one account: the AOV increase did not improve profitability, it merely let the brand keep affording the platform. This is the operator-side consequence of MD-035, which banks the platform-side mechanism (Meta captures its own efficiency gains through CPMs). Diagnostic: build a multi-year table of spend, impressions, reach, frequency, CTR, add-to-cart rate, CPA and ROAS, and compare year against year rather than reading any single window.

Planning application, arithmetic rather than data: if cost per thousand reached is up 50% year over year, doubling Black Friday revenue needs more than double the spend. The compounding factor is the owned-audience side. Brands spend all year assuming they are building an email list and a retargetable pool, then arrive at Black Friday to find the list has not grown at the same rate or has shrunk through unsubscribes, while returning customers make up a larger share of BFCM revenue every year. The peak-season plan cannot be built off last year's spend-to-revenue ratio.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Your Meta Ads Account Has Too Many Campaigns: Here's Why And What To Do, 2026-05-12
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-120 · Profitable scaling margin is LTV over final CPA, and because CPA is a function of spend level, a healthy CPA today does not survive the budget you plan to reach
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Charley T defines profitable scaling margin as LTV divided by final CPA. A 1.73 reading means 73 cents of profit for every dollar spent, measured against customer LTV rather than first-order revenue. He then raises modelled spend 40% and shows final CPA climbing to $142, which compresses the margin toward break-even. The operating point is the one most budget plans skip: CPA is not a constant you inherit, it rises with the spend level, so the margin has to be modelled AT the target budget before the budget is approved. Practitioner model worked on screen, no exported account data. This is the unit-economics form of the same curve behind [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-014|MM-014]] (ratios fall as scale rises) and it is what makes a forecast built off today's CPA dishonest.

Second operator on the mechanism, with the reason named: "as you scale, your return on ad spend will decrease, inevitably. It is harder, it is more expensive, to acquire your 10,000th customer than your 50th customer, because there's always a proportion of the market that is just so keen and loves whatever it is that you sell." The highly motivated share of any market is finite and gets consumed first, so the curve is a property of the demand pool rather than of delivery or creative. Practical consequence for forecasting: the decay is expected and must be built into the model, so a plan that holds today's CPA flat across a 40% budget increase is not conservative, it is wrong. Asserted, no data.
Sources: Professor Charley T, Q5: the 2nd Black Friday: Meta Ads for Profit, 2025-12-20; Ben Heath, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026, 2026-02-20
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-121 · Profit equals (blended AOV minus blended CPA) times transaction volume, which leaves exactly three levers: repeat transactions, CPA, and AOV
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Charley T's replacement equation for ROAS, stated across two videos. Gross profit per transaction is AOV minus CPA; business profit is that gap times total transactions. Three levers follow. Raise repeat transactions, which lowers effective CPA across the customer's life. Lower CPA directly through cheaper channels, and he names retargeting, catalogue, email and search. Or raise AOV. His argument is that most brands push on CPA and ROAS while AOV is both the fastest unlock and the one that does not decay, since a CPA win fatigues and an AOV win does not. He also uses the equation to justify catalogue ads specifically, on the basis that they suppress blended CPA by adding transaction volume rather than by getting cheaper per unit. Stated as a definition with illustrative arithmetic, no data shown. This is the algebra behind [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-080|MM-080]]'s GPT metric and behind [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-014|MM-014]]'s profit-dollars doctrine.
Sources: Professor Charley T, How to Scale Facebook Ads in 2026, 2026-02-28; Professor Charley T, How to Scale Profits with Meta Ads, 2026-01-24

### MM-122 · When a product's AOV sits below the cost cap you want to run, fix it off-platform by raising price, re-merchandising, adding an in-cart upsell, or moving the free-shipping threshold
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Faris's point is that restructuring the media buying is only step one. A product producing a $60 AOV against a $50 cap is an offer problem, and the first lever is price: test $60 to $70, since a $10 rise adds roughly 10% to the margin profile, which then buys room to raise the CAC target itself. The other levers he lists all sit outside Ads Manager. Reorder the collection page the ad points at and pull sold-out items. Add an in-cart upsell tied to that specific product at a 20% discount. Move the free-shipping or free-gift threshold, his example being a $75 threshold sitting above a $60 AOV product, which is the mean-versus-distribution error in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-089|MM-089]]. The second-order benefit is that products converge on similar AOV, which lets you re-consolidate them into fewer ad sets. Scope limit he states himself: do this on the high spenders, not on the number four product carrying 10% of account spend. Reasoning, no test shown.
Sources: Andrew Faris, How To Fix The Most Common Bid Cap & Cost Cap Mistake I See, 2026-06-25

## Cash, Inventory, and Forecasting

### MM-123 · Split the catalogue into two lanes, highest combined two-purchase AOV for long-term value and fastest time to second purchase for cash flow, because they select different SKUs
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The same repeat-purchase data sorted two ways produces two different shortlists, and Charley T treats both as valid for different jobs. On one apparel store the highest combined two-purchase AOV pairs sat at $377 and $260 but took 145 to 401 days to complete, while the fastest-repeat SKUs completed in 19, 24 and 30 days at materially lower AOV. He labels the first lane long-term value and the second lane cash-flow devices, and his instruction on the first lane is to bundle the high-AOV pairs into a single offer rather than wait 400 days for the sequence to complete naturally.

The cash lane is decided by supplier terms, which is the part that makes this a finance decision rather than a merchandising one. On a supplement account he contrasts a product taking about 50 days to repeat and worth about $57 across both sales against a peptide repeating in 3 days and worth almost twice as much: on net 30 or net 60 payment terms, a 3-day repeat cycle recovers the cash before the bill lands. He makes the same point from the other side, that 60-day supplier terms are what let you afford to lose money on an entry product. Choose the lane from the cash conversion cycle in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-074|MM-074]]: a business financing inventory needs the fast lane regardless of which SKU carries the higher lifetime value. Real store data shown on screen, no controlled test. Selection method itself is banked at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-079|MM-079]].
Sources: Professor Charley T, If you run Facebook Ads... everything just changed., 2025-12-06; Professor Charley T, Copy this Meta Ad strategy to BLOW UP your business. (After Andromeda, Clothing Niche), 2026-01-31; Professor Charley T, Mastering Andromeda: Creative Testing and Scaling, 2025-12-13
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-074 · The cash conversion cycle, not marketing efficiency, is the growth limiter in inventory e-commerce
Tier: T3 · Status: active
CCC = DIO + DSO - DPO. The mechanism: gross profit from one purchase order only funds a marginally larger next PO, and a 90-day manufacturing lead time with prepayment terms forces stockouts between batches. Levers: negotiate deposit terms (20% up front instead of 100%), shorten manufacturer lead time, or take inventory financing, which usually requires personal collateral. He reports the same failure five times. An agency ramps spend from $20k to $200k/month, the client quietly takes inventory financing to keep up, interest repayments plus degraded MER compress net margin toward zero, and a $2M business becomes a $7M business making no money while the founder's income goes to zero. Practical consequences: you cannot triple an inventory business in a year, an agency that scales spend without inventory visibility can take a cash-positive business to zero net profit, and growing slower at better efficiency with less debt often produces more profit. ASSERTED from client work, no numbers shown.
**The earliest statement of this thesis on the roster, and it adds the timing mechanism the entries above do not carry.** Recorded 2025-01-23, fourteen months before the two sources previously on this claim. His model: $10,000 of stock plus $5,000 of ads returns $20,000 at a 2x price-to-cost multiple and a 4x efficiency ratio, on a $15,000 founding injection and a roughly 60-day cycle. Cycle two buys $14,000 of stock, spends $7,000 on ads and ends at $27,000 of cash. All of that looks healthy.

**Then he moves the purchase order to when it actually has to be placed, which is roughly 30 days BEFORE the current cycle's revenue lands, because otherwise you stock out while the factory runs.** The same point in the cycle now holds $20,000 less $14,000 = **$6,000**, and the next $7,000 of advertising has to come out of that $6,000. **The business is short before a single operating expense is counted, and the steady-state bank balance is $6,000 against the $15,000 that was put in.** Push the efficiency ratio down to the 2x a new brand without product-market fit actually achieves and the ad spend needed to move the same stock becomes $14,000, at which point there is no money left at all. Every figure recomputes exactly as stated.

**The financing ladder, which explains why the debt in the paragraph above is so expensive.** A company loan requires a full financial year of statements to satisfy a lender, which a first-year brand does not have, so the realistic instrument is a merchant cash advance at a stated **35% to 350%**. (The company-loan range is garbled in the auto-transcript as "17 to 16%" and is not usable; the merchant cash advance figure is clean.) His established-client case: a target of **$750,000 over the 60-day November-December window at a 3x price-to-cost multiple requires about $250,000 of stock**, financed at roughly 20%, which is **about $4,200 a month** and **$8,000 to $12,000** over the 60 to 90 days it takes to pay down. At a 20% to 25% net margin that is a good trade, **and only if the revenue target is near-certain.** He names both failure branches himself: land at $400,000 instead and roughly $100,000 of the loan cannot be paid down, or hit the $750,000 but at a 2.1x efficiency ratio instead of 4x, so the free cash flow that was meant to clear the loan went back into the next purchase order.

**The consequence he draws is aimed at agencies, and it is why this sits in our codex rather than in a finance note: an aggressive growth projection is a request that the client take on debt.** The client rarely says so out loud. Our own version of the same failure is already recorded above from his 2026 material, where an agency ramps spend and the client quietly finances the inventory behind it.

His closing line is the cleanest statement of the mechanism anywhere in this claim: **the cash conversion cycle does not start at the first dollar of ad spend, it starts at the first dollar of inventory purchase.** Read alongside [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-189|MM-189]], which says advertising sits outside the accounting formula and lengthens the real cycle. Those are the two ends of the same clock. Illustrative model plus one asserted client case, no documents shown.

Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04; Blue Sense Digital, Why eCommerce Is So Difficult (Deep Dive), 2025-01-23
Last touched: 2026-09-05

### MM-075 · Paid media is an inventory liquidation tool: push grade B/C/D stock at breakeven or a slight loss to convert balance-sheet inventory back into cash
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Shopify auto-ranks inventory A (fast moving, worry about stockouts) through D (zero sell-through) under the products tab. Product launches fail often, so cash piles up as stock: a brand can show $2M of P&L profit while holding $3M of unsold inventory and be $1M cash-negative. An agency KPI'd purely on profit will never touch grade C/D because the ROAS is bad, so the marketing team needs inventory visibility and an explicit mandate. Tactics: isolate the dead SKU in its own funnel, which does not even need to sit on the main site (whitelisting pages work), or push it into a different country where heavy discounting will not erode brand equity at home.

The CFO reflex in this situation is the wrong move twice over. Seeing MER spike and profit dip, the instinct is to cut marketing 40%, which drops the revenue the marketing was driving, worsens profitability, and invites a further cut. That loop is distinct from the CAC-creep spiral in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-004|MM-004]]. Two things break it: recognising that in a marketing-driven business the compression is an efficiency problem on current spend rather than a spend-volume problem, and recognising that the underlying cash problem is unsold stock that needs MORE spend at worse efficiency to clear. His worked move is raising MER from 30% to 35% specifically to buy the sell-through that restores the cash position.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-076 · Allocate fashion budget by days of inventory, not by ROAS, because Pareto compounds and the algorithm funds the product that would have sold anyway
Tier: T3 · Status: active
His stated pattern: roughly 4% of products and 4% of ads drive 64% of revenue. Worked example, three products, 10,000 units each, $100k monthly budget. Product A has gone semi-viral at 55% monthly sell-through with 50 days of inventory and a 7x ROAS, so 90% of budget flows to it and compresses its days on inventory to 9. That product was going to clear in 50 days regardless, so $90k of spend converted roughly $500k of gross profit into roughly $410k of contribution margin to buy speed the business did not need. Meanwhile the products at 75 and 240 days of inventory get nothing and end up marked down. Correct allocation puts spend behind the 4.4x and 2.5x products to pull their days on inventory down, accepting near-zero contribution on those orders, because the alternative is markdown or write-off. Ties directly to [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-075|MM-075]].
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-077 · Forecast new-customer and returning-customer revenue by two separate methods: regression for new, cohort repeat curves for returning
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Stated as the number one rule of DTC forecasting, because the two lines have different drivers. New revenue is driven by ad spend, influencers and events. Returning revenue is driven by product launches, discount periods, activations and email/SMS.

Returning method: for every historical acquisition cohort, take the cohort size, look up the average repeat rate at the month of life it is about to enter (for example 0.1% at month 32), multiply by returning-customer AOV, and sum across all cohorts. He claims this lands within about 10%, and within 1-2% after two refinements: a seasonality factor built from three years of returning-customer revenue, then one-off marketing events superimposed that did not exist last year. Accuracy figures are ASSERTED, not demonstrated.

New method: plot daily acquisition MER on the y axis against daily spend on the x axis for the last year, delete sale-period and November/BFCM points because they overstate business-as-usual efficiency, delete days below the minimum realistic spend, and fit a logarithmic or linear regression by best fit. To forecast, enter at the acquisition MER the revenue goal requires, read across to the required spend, and check whether the account can physically spend that much. The real job of the exercise is exposing the gap between the achievable number and the board target, which then has to be closed by a named input (new channel, product launch, tripled creative production) rather than by hope.

Worked example of that last step, from a Blue Sense planning session. Start at a 60% growth target over 12 months and ask what has to be true. The answer given was three named inputs: a second product, because every competitor sells one and the single-SKU catalogue caps the number; doubling or tripling ad production, because current volume is nowhere near the target; and a 100% budget increase roadmapped against the P&L so the increase can actually be substantiated. The point of stating them is that daily reactive micro-changes in the account push one-percenters and cannot reach a 60% number, so a target that is not backed by a named structural input is not a plan. No client numbers shown, presented as the planning method.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Everything You Need to Know About Finance in eCommerce, 2026-05-04; Blue Sense Digital, The Future of Google Ads, AI & Agencies (with Caden), 2025-06-19
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-078 · Deliberate contraction can be the profit move: a 65% year-over-year Q4 revenue cut to right-size unit economics produced a multi-million-dollar EBITDA swing
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The brand carried "low seven figures" of lost EBITDA plus balance-sheet debt, ran zero ad spend from March to September 2025, restarted in late October under a first-order-profit mandate, and was at all-time revenue highs by Q1 2026. February and March 2026 together beat all of Q1 2024, at roughly twice the profitability. The exact EBITDA swing was bleeped at the guest's request; the 65% Q4 decline and the "twice the profitability" figures are stated openly. Faris notes Q4 scale was also inventory-constrained, so the decline was not purely chosen. Read from an internal doc on a podcast, nothing shown. Pair with [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-003|MM-003]]: this is what reverting to first-order profitability costs and returns.
Sources: Andrew Faris (with Richie Mashiko), From A 65% Decline To All-Time Revenue & Profit Highs, 2026-04-27
