### MM-031 · Subscription only retains when the delivery mechanism itself serves the customer, which is why supplement subscriptions work and forced memberships fail
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A daily pill has an obvious 30-day replenishment cycle, so auto-shipment genuinely improves the product experience and produces high retention. Brands that bolt on subscriptions or membership programs nobody wants sink effort into a black hole. One client discovered an unexpectedly high-retention subscription product (not a supplement) and is reorganizing the whole business around it; the filter is whether recurring delivery plays a real role in the customer's life.

Where subscription does work, the advantage is real but partly a TIMING effect, and Faris gives both halves. The advantage: "the subscription customers, across every data set I've ever seen, come back at a much higher rate than non-subscription customers... I've never seen a data set show otherwise." The catch: "at some point it's possible that your subscribers and your non-subscribers have revenue curves that converge on one another because at some point somebody has too many of the product that you've sold them and they're going to cancel their subscription." Three named mechanisms for the convergence: subscribers accumulate excess product and cancel, non-subscribers who see results keep buying or convert to subscription later, and some repeat demand migrates to Amazon where the brand cannot observe it at all.

The operating rule that falls out, live on a current client: three months of subscriber cohort data is not enough to justify scaling spend into that segment, because the convergence shows up after month three and the decision cannot be unwound six months later. "Before we go hammer money and just pound money into that group, we're getting a little more aggressive and then we're going to watch months 4, months 5, months 6." Raise spend modestly, then read months four to six, then commit. Both halves asserted from his own book, no data set shown.
Sources: Andrew Faris, You're Working Hard On The Wrong Problem. Here's How To Tell., 2026-07-27; Andrew Faris, A DEEP Dive On How Retention Works For Ecommerce Businesses, 2026-04-21
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-032 · Non-subscription email/retention work is front-loaded: a 2-6 week agency sprint, then one person maintains it in ~15 minutes a day with AI
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Pattern across portfolio brands: bring a technically strong email agency in for a 2-6 week sprint (deliverability, suppression cadences, IP rotation between welcome and promo flows, flow setup), then a single doer churns out campaigns from AI-modified templates while watching open and click rates, roughly 15 minutes a day. Full-time in-house retention hires are a trap for non-subscription brands; re-audit technically about every 6 months.
Sources: Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), The AI Traps Wasting Your Time In An Ecommerce Brand, 2026-08-03
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-033 · Renting vs owning demand: new-customer acquisition is always rented attention; owned channels only compound the LTGP side
Tier: T3 · Status: active
You rarely own new-customer acquisition; you rent attention, acquire, then own the first-party data for remarketing. Even organic/personal-brand reach is unreliable ownership: a 50k-follower account rarely gets a post shown to that audience without effectively re-renting distribution. So "get off paid ads" dogma is wrong for acquisition, but owned channels are the correct lever for returning-customer revenue.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Death Spiral That Kills Most eCommerce Brands, 2026-07-22
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-034 · First-party database accuracy is the durable targeting edge: Tesco's Clubcard won because mailing coupons forced accurate addresses, enabling surgical response to competitor store openings
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Tesco mailed discounts instead of redeeming at the till, which forced customers to keep an accurate address on file. When a competitor opened nearby and a store's usage dropped, Tesco could deploy coupons against exactly the defecting households instead of spraying the region. The mechanism, accurate identity data in your database enabling person-level response, is unchanged in modern retargeting and Meta business agents.
Sources: Ben Heath, Meta's CMO on AI Ads and Rising Meta Ad Costs, 2026-07-20
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-104 · Discount-takers have structurally lower LTV, driven mainly by a collapsed second-purchase rate rather than by the margin given away on the first order
Tier: T4 · Status: active
The real cost of a discount-led acquisition offer is the destroyed back end, which never appears in first-order reporting. Stated as universal ("that goes for every business") with no data behind it, so tier accordingly. Supports [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-024|MM-024]] with a retention mechanism and supports [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-030|MM-030]] by naming the discount as the offending offer variable. Operator move: split cohort retention by whether the first order carried a discount code before concluding a discount-led acquisition strategy is working.
Sources: Professor Charley T, The BEST Facebook Ads Strategy for 2026 Post Andromeda, 2025-12-27
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-105 · A recurring $20/month membership front end ascends worse than a one-time purchase of $300 or less
Tier: T3 · Status: active
From a low-ticket business he built and exited (a $20/month subscription that reached seven figures with no back end for years) plus current client accounts. Claimed mechanism is buyer psychology: someone still being billed monthly resists a second purchase, while a one-time buyer has closed the loop and can be sold again. Implication for ascension ladders off paid traffic: prefer a one-time front-end price under about $300 over an equivalent-LTV subscription when the goal is moving people to a high-ticket call. He labels it explicitly as his own experience and tells his co-host to test it, and it runs against the usual "MRR compounds" default, so treat it as a hypothesis to test per account rather than a rule.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, Organic vs Paid Ads: What's Actually Working In 2026, 2026-06-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-158 · Returning customers were discounted 42% against 22% on acquisition, and matching the acquisition rate moves returning profit contribution from $29,000 to $52,000 a month
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The finding is that the discount is pointed the wrong way round. On the account at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-010|MM-010]], the cohort with no acquisition cost attached was being discounted nearly twice as deeply as the cohort the business is paying to buy. That halves returning gross margin to 38% and cuts returning AOV by 40 to 50%. Fixing only the discount rate: "what if we just matched acquisition at 22% discounting? Gross profit jumps to $63 and returning customer profit contribution just jumped to 52,000 from 29, and so that is a $23,000 jump in profit per month, just by fixing the discount rate within this business." Modelled on the real baseline, not implemented, so the $52,000 is an output and only the $29,000 and the two discount rates are observed.

Why the returning cohort punches above its volume: it carries no direct marketing cost, so gross profit flows straight to contribution. In this account returning orders were one third of volume at roughly half the gross profit per order and still produced $29,000 against $34,000 from first-time orders. He generalises it, and the generalisation is the transferable part: "despite returning customers doing one third of the volume and having significantly worse gross profit, you actually make almost more money on returning customers, in most cases you do make more money on returning customers, because there's no advertising cost associated with them."

The diagnostic that finds this in any account: returning gross retail SHOULD be higher than first-time gross retail, because a customer who has already received the product and had a good experience buys with more confidence. "What you should always end up saying is that you should have higher gross retail... in this case they don't, so really interesting, average unit retail is slightly lower." Where returning gross retail is lower, read it as a retention-strategy problem or a portfolio problem, typically a catalogue thin enough that the only way to trigger a repeat purchase is to discount. That is [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-030|MM-030]] arriving from the finance side: the fix is product and offer, not a better email flow. Companion to [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-071|MM-071]], which handles the spend that leaks onto this cohort; this handles the margin the business gives away to it voluntarily.

Data-quality note, recorded so nobody banks the headline. The same video's CLOSING arithmetic does not reconcile with its own earlier figures. The two cohort contributions stated on screen, $34,000 first-time and $29,000 returning, sum to $63,000, and he then talks about a figure closer to 35 to 40, before closing on a monthly profit number around $100,000. Those three cannot all be true of one account in one month. The $100,000 close is NOT merged into this codex and must not be quoted from it. Only the per-cohort figures, the two discount rates and the modelled $52,000 are carried, and the modelled figure is labelled as such above.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-155 · For most brands, including high-LTV ones, more contribution margin comes from cutting CAC than from chasing retention, and the CAC-versus-volume trade is non-linear
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Relay of Taylor Holiday, endorsed by Faris after reviewing more data sets of his own: "Taylor basically said brands who are high LTV brands do not take enough stock of their ROAS on first purchase... when you look at the actual charts, that actually isn't the way to drive the most contribution margin in your business, that even for super high LTV brands, more contribution margin comes via driving your CAC down than most people realize." The charts are referenced, not shown. Faris names his own exception: categories where scale itself unlocks the margin, food and beverage in particular, where volume buys the cost structure that makes the business work.

The counterweight he supplies himself, and it stops this being read as "cut CAC": "there's a non-linear trade-off between scale and CAC. You can get your CAC down lower, but you might lose way more scale. So you might get 10% better CAC, but 40% less volume, and that trade might actually not be worth making." The 10% and 40% are invented for the illustration. Put together, the operating question is not whether to cut CAC but what volume the cut costs, which is exactly the profit-dollars test in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-014|MM-014]] applied in the efficient direction rather than the expansionary one.

Where it lands in the standing argument at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-003|MM-003]]: this is a data-informed voice on the Blue Sense side, saying first-purchase efficiency matters even for the businesses that have genuinely earned the right to ignore it. It does not settle the contest, because the charts behind it were never shown.
Sources: Andrew Faris, A DEEP Dive On How Retention Works For Ecommerce Businesses, 2026-04-21
Last touched: 2026-08-20

## Conversion Math and CRO Economics

### MM-035 · Conversion rate is an output of ~50 inputs and is gameable; the operating metric should be revenue per visitor (CR x AOV) or contribution per visitor
Tier: T3 · Status: active
CR can be inflated overnight by cutting cold spend (warm traffic converts ~4x cold), so it detaches from revenue. Example: a 9% CR store at $45-55 AOV is a worse business than a 1% CR store doing $200M at 50% contribution margin. His agency tracks RPV over a 2.5-year window instead of CR. Shopify metric fixes: use net sales + shipping collected (shipping is realized revenue Shopify excludes from AOV), and use GA4 sessions because Shopify under-filters bot traffic.

Restated 2026-08-20 with the correction stated in both directions: true AOV ADDS shipping collected and REMOVES the return rate, and the resulting figure "will actually be significantly higher than Shopify's number because it includes shipping collected which it obviously should, because that is cash collected at checkout that gets paid out to you." Two consequences. Any AOV read straight off Shopify understates the money the business actually banks per order, which flows into the affordable CAC. And any AOV that has not had returns taken out overstates it, which is acute in fashion. Both corrections have to be applied, not one.

The formula behind the correction, stated 2025-07-22 and added here for completeness: Shopify's reported AOV is gross sales minus discounts divided by orders. That excludes shipping collected at checkout, which the host puts at roughly 10% of revenue for most brands with no source given, and it ignores refunds entirely. The formula was spoken from memory rather than read off a documentation page, so it is practitioner recall and not documentation. Check it against Shopify's own definition before quoting it. The 10% figure is the same order of magnitude as the shipping-collected band in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-025|MM-025]], from the same source, so it is one operator's number stated twice rather than two.

Scoring rule for the tests this metric governs, from a fourth operator. Score a price test, a shipping-threshold test or a first-purchase-discount test on PROFIT per visitor, never on revenue per visitor and never on conversion rate, because all three levers trade conversion rate against average order value and either single metric can move the right way while profit falls. That extends the claim above from a reporting metric to a test-scoring rule. The measurement principle is sound and transferable. It arrives inside a paid sponsor read with a discount code attached, so carry the principle and none of the product.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, eCommerce CRO Masterclass 2026: The Full System, 2026-08-10; Blue Sense Digital, How to Double Your Profit Using Levers in Your Unit Economics, 2025-04-01; Blue Sense Digital, How To Breakdown Shopify Data Into Country Unit Economics with Valentin, 2025-07-22; Andrew Faris, Build A Culture Of Truth-Telling In Your Business, 2026-05-22
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-036 · Blended conversion rate mechanically declines as cold traffic share grows; big brands have lower CR by construction
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Worked example: blended CR 2.8% decomposes into cold Meta traffic converting at 1% and warm high-intent traffic at 4%; turn Meta off and CR jumps to 4 overnight while revenue collapses. As cold scale increases, blended CR converges toward the cold rate, so comparing CR across brands or periods without traffic-mix context is meaningless. What to read INSTEAD when a new channel is switched on is banked at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-147|MM-147]]: the absolute count of completed sessions, which mix cannot explain away.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, eCommerce CRO Masterclass 2026: The Full System, 2026-08-10
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-037 · CRO priority order by ICE: clarity/hierarchy, copy, offer, proof, friction, with offer as the single highest-impact lever
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A bad offer holds CR near 0% and a great one can take it to 80%; nothing in clarity, copy, proof, or friction has that range. But offer curation is hard, so the quick-win order starts at above-the-fold clarity. The same list doubles as landing-page structure top to bottom. Above the fold must answer four questions: what is it, why this one (unique mechanism), why now, why trust you.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, eCommerce CRO Masterclass 2026: The Full System, 2026-08-10
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-038 · Going from 0 to ~80-100 reviews roughly doubles conversion rate; beyond ~80 the effect is immeasurable
Tier: T3 · Status: active
From running his own e-commerce brands in 2019-2020, adding reviews was the single easiest CR doubler. The jump happens from 0 to ~80; 80 vs 150 vs 400 reads the same to a consumer. Auto-sort reviews with photos/videos first and incentivize photo reviews (gift card or $10 cashback, where legal) because image/video reviews carry far more trust than text. Proof must match the specific claim being advertised: five hyper-relevant reviews beat 400 generic ones.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, eCommerce CRO Masterclass 2026: The Full System, 2026-08-10
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-039 · Cart abandonment breakdown (Baymard): ~70% abandon; the fixable causes are extra costs 39%, slow delivery 21%, card trust 19%, forced account 19%, long checkout 18%, returns policy 15%, hidden total 14%
Tier: T2 · Status: active
43% of abandoners were only browsing and are unrecoverable. Fixes mapped: keep checkout add-ons under 10% of AOV; test hiding delivery dates if genuinely slow; enable guest checkout (Shopify may default to forced accounts); enable Shop Pay/Apple Pay/Google Pay and strip form fields; show total cost upfront. One-page checkout converts slightly better than multi-step in their data. Caution: free returns can be net-negative in fashion via return-rate abuse.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, eCommerce CRO Masterclass 2026: The Full System, 2026-08-10
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-040 · A brand-new store sending ads direct to a Shopify PDP with no pre-lander hit $850 on day 1 and $1,000-1,200/day by day 3-4 at a tracked 1.54% conversion rate
Tier: T2 · Status: active
The operator deliberately skipped his usual funnel (ad, advertorial pre-lander, sales page, order page, upsell path) and ran ad-to-PDP on a free Debut theme, fully on Shopify, on a new pixel and new ad account. Day 1: $850; account shut down day 2; restored on the 19th, first $1k day; $1.2k the next day. Conclusion: creatives plus a solid lander drive results; theme, homepage design, and funnel software are mostly noise.

Boundary from Nick Theriot, which scopes the case rather than contradicting it: where the ad deliberately opens a very cold demographic, sending that traffic straight to the product page underperforms and a bridge asset is needed, his examples being an advertorial, a quiz funnel or a listicle. The case above ran a direct-response product to a PDP for buyers already close to the category. The variable is audience temperature, not funnel software, which is the same distinction [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-036|MM-036]] makes about conversion rate. Asserted, no numbers.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, f*ck it. ZERO to $1k/day in 3 days with ai dropshipping. [full case study], 2026-07-30; Nick Theriot, Sh*t I care about when scaling Facebook Ads, 2026-07-10
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-147 · When a new channel is switched on, read the ABSOLUTE count of completed sessions, not the conversion rate: sessions rose 63% while sessions-completed fell 969 to 721
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Shown on the Shopify behaviour reports for the account in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-146|MM-146]]. Adding TikTok lifted sessions 63%, about 11,000 extra sessions against 9,800 TikTok clicks, so nearly all the new traffic is identifiable as the new channel's. Sessions with cart additions fell in absolute terms. Reached-checkout fell further. Sessions that completed checkout fell from 969 to 721. Site conversion rate fell 54%.

The instrument is the discrimination, not the conversion-rate drop. A falling blended conversion rate on its own proves nothing, because [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-036|MM-036]] holds that the rate declines mechanically as cold traffic share rises, which is what healthy scaling looks like. What mix cannot explain is the ABSOLUTE count of completed sessions falling while total sessions rise. That combination means the new traffic converts at or near zero and the rest of the funnel got no better, so the channel is adding traffic and subtracting profit. Confirmation on the way out: switching TikTok off produced no revenue drop and conversion rate went 1.5% to 8% overnight, settling near 6%. The speaker flags his own confound, that a sale launched at the same moment, so 6% is the honest read and the 8% spike is contaminated.

Operator form, before any budget increase on a new channel: put absolute sessions-completed, absolute add-to-carts and absolute reached-checkouts beside the previous period. If any fell in absolute terms, the channel is not additive whatever the platform reports, and the correct move is off rather than up. Cost of skipping this check is in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-146|MM-146]].
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Be Careful: TikTok Ads Nearly Bankrupted This Business, 2025-03-24
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-106 · A low conversion rate is an indicator of a constraint, never the constraint itself, and it is only a real problem when it sits below the category benchmark
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Drawn from weekly numbers forms filled in by members of his program, where the recurring pattern is healthy CPM, CPC and CTR sitting next to a bad conversion rate. Conversion rate only tells you people arrived and did not buy, never why, so the diagnosis has to come from step-by-step funnel KPIs. His example: an extreme checkout-page drop-off traced to a payment processor nobody had configured. The paired pre-launch rule is stronger than standard QA. Do not quality-check the funnel, actively try to break it before launch, because otherwise customers will.

Second half, from Professor Charley T: benchmark the page against the category before doing any CRO. At 3% against a category norm of 1%, or at an astronomical 10%, the page is not the bottleneck and the correct move is more spend. Below 1%, no volume of traffic rescues it, because you cannot scale broken math, and the fix is either traffic quality or what happens after the click. Tripling traffic while halving conversion rate still makes more money. Illustrative arithmetic, no account data. Together these turn [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-035|MM-035]] and [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-036|MM-036]] into a routing rule.

One unsupported benchmark logged here rather than banked as a rule, because it is specific enough to be worth recognising if it shows up elsewhere. Blue Sense asserts a 4:2:1 ratio for add to cart, initiate checkout and purchase: "is your add to cart to initiate checkout to purchase ratio a 4-2-1? If not, troubleshoot it. Needs to be a 4-2-1. If it's not, something's wrong that you can fix. I'm not going to tell you how to fix it in this video." No mechanism, no data, no source for the number, and he explicitly declines to give the remedy. It is also in obvious tension with the step-by-step diagnosis above, which says a ratio tells you people dropped and never why. Do not troubleshoot against it. Treat a departure from 4:2:1 as a place to look, never as evidence that something is broken.

A second unsupported ladder, logged the same way and from a different operator, because having two on file is more useful than having one. Mark Builds Brands: site conversion rate 3% to 5%, scaled inversely to price, with cheaper products at 5% and $90 to $100 products at 3%. Add-to-cart rate 10%. Add-to-cart to purchase 30%, and below that he localizes the problem to the cart or checkout page, which is the same move as the payment-processor example above. All ASSERTED, no account shown. The internal arithmetic holds, since 10% add-to-cart times 30% is 3% purchase. Note where the two ladders disagree: 4:2:1 puts add-to-cart to purchase at 25%, this one at 30%, and neither is sourced, so the honest read is that the true figure sits somewhere in a band nobody has measured on camera. The price-scaling half is the only piece here with a stated mechanism, and it is the useful piece, because it says a 3% conversion rate on a $95 product and a 3% on a $20 product are not the same result.

The pre-launch habit that goes with this, from the same operator and matching the break-the-funnel rule above: he buys from every new funnel himself before it sees cold traffic, as a standing quality-control step rather than a one-off check.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21; Professor Charley T, The NEW BEST Meta Ads Andromeda Course to Scale in 2026, 2026-01-24; Blue Sense Digital, 5 Step Checklist to Increase Meta Ad Performance Overnight, 2025-02-26; Mark Builds Brands, how to turn any product into a money printer w branded dropshipping, 2026-02-17
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-136 · A 10% conversion-rate improvement is worth more than any single creative win, because it multiplies every ad on every channel and never fatigues
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Charley T's argument for spending time on the funnel instead of inside Ads Manager. A 10% lift in site conversion rate makes every ad 10% better overnight, across all channels, permanently, while a creative win decays as the audience saturates. Post-click improvements do not fatigue. He uses it to justify a low-intervention approach to the ad account: the hours not spent fiddling with campaigns go into the thing that compounds.

Reconcile it with his own routing rule in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-106|MM-106]], which says to benchmark the page against the category first and, if you are already above the category norm, spend more rather than run CRO. The two are compatible read in order: benchmark first, and if there is genuine headroom, the CR lever outranks the creative lever on durability. The honest caveat is that a 10% CR lift is not a small ask, and AT-027 holds that most reported 10-20% CRO wins are noise absent roughly 1,000 conversions per variant. Reasoning, no test shown.
Sources: Professor Charley T, How to Scale Facebook Ads in 2026, 2026-02-28
Last touched: 2026-08-19

## Product and Market Selection

### MM-041 · Winning products are created by marketing and funnel quality, not found; the best-marketed product beats the best product
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Brands scale one-ingredient supplements to eight figures per month, showing the product itself is not the differentiator. What looks like striking a winning product is usually a product that was easier to market plus good creatives and a good funnel. Two sellers of the identical product get opposite results purely on marketing skill.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, f*ck it. ZERO to $1k/day in 3 days with ai dropshipping. [full case study], 2026-07-30
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-042 · Product selection criteria: a painful problem in a passionate market, shoebox-sized logistics (worldwide shipping under ~$5), and LTV potential via upsells or subscriptions
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The deeper the pain on Maslow's hierarchy (health/safety level), the easier the sell, and the market must either buy constantly (weight loss) or be a deep niche passion. Anything that fits a shoebox ships anywhere for roughly under $5, avoiding logistical issues. Subscriptions raise CAC but build recurring revenue; the operating thesis is "whoever can spend the most to acquire a new customer wins." Total product research should take under ~2 hours.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, f*ck it. ZERO to $1k/day in 3 days with ai dropshipping. [full case study], 2026-07-30
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-043 · Avatar brands trade TAM for dominance: a smaller market fully owned beats a sliver of a mass market
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Gym Shark launched 2012 into the emerging lifter avatar Nike/Reebok/Under Armour ignored (Google Trends bump ~2010-2013) and hit a $1B valuation by 2020, versus Nike's $61B in 2026. The mechanism: the more accurately the customer sees themselves reflected in the brand, the less it feels sold to them, so the niche brand captures ALL of its segment's spend rather than a fraction of the mass market.

Same thesis under the name "purple ocean": enter a market with proven demand first, then carve out a hyper-specific segment and aim for 100% of it. Cases: Primal Queen sold beef organ supplements specifically to postpartum and menopausal women inside the carnivore trend (claimed 4.5M searches/month peak, January 2025, per Google Trends) instead of a general carnivore supplement; a nootropics brand sold specifically to entrepreneurs; a pillowcase brand positioned against overnight acne rather than sleep quality. The segment does not have to be demographic, it can be a single angle. Sequence: verify demand in the parent market, then find the sub-segment with no dedicated competitor.

Rare reversal case in favour of the same rule: Fashionphile added an entry-tier brand (Coach) to widen the funnel on a stated gateway-drug hypothesis, expecting buyers to move up to Chanel and Hermes over time. Existing customers objected to sifting through it. Curation itself was the product, so diluting the assortment degraded the browse experience, and the tier was withdrawn. The entry customer is now reached through separate channels rather than by broadening the catalog. Funnel-widening can cost the core segment's trust faster than it converts anyone upward.

CONTESTED by Mehtab Bhogal, who argues the opposite selection rule post-iOS/ATT: broad-TAM products in dense, heavily competed categories now outperform niche products because the algorithms have more data to optimize on, and thin categories are thin for a reason. He dates the break to the loss of manual targeting precision and paid-search niche wins, and says niche CAC has risen to the point where those products are hard to sell online. Neither side showed data; both are reasoning from their own portfolios. The plausible reconciliation is [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-045|MM-045]]: broad TAM measured in category entry points, executed with hyper-specific persona creative.
Sources: Nick Theriot, The Most Braindead Way To Find Winning Products in 2026 (this will get banned), 2026-08-17; Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21; Andrew Faris, Fashionphile's 27 Year Journey To $400M, 2026-05-29; Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), One Packaging Tweak, $375K In Profit, 2026-06-18
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-044 · Opportunity selection framework: gross margin, retention rate, TAM, unique mechanism; two or more present makes a strong brand, none means change products or quit
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Score products on four levers: high gross margin, high retention rate, large TAM, and a genuinely unique product/mechanism. Most brands have at most one; two or more of the four is the recipe for outsized scale. If a product has none, the honest options are: develop new products designed to add a lever, stop working on it, or deliberately play a hard category where difficulty itself keeps competitors out (e.g. Simple Modern winning Amazon drinkware by taking lower margin and more colors up front).

Fifth lever added 2026-08-18: intellectual property. Bhogal rates IP the strongest product moat because its operating leverage rises with volume while manufacturing carries the same marginal cost forever. He names Ridge's wallet IP portfolio as the underdiscussed reason they could scale off one core product far longer than expected, and notes nobody tells the Ridge story that way. First-to-market is the weak moat, because copycats erode the margin. He also flags the counter-strategy of a brand that refuses all publicity precisely because it has no IP to defend a copied position. This gives [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-046|MM-046]] its exception mechanism.

His new-SKU launch checklist extends the same framework into a launch decision: product-channel fit first, then whether the SKU fixes an existing weakness in the business, TAM per channel, IP defensibility, margin structure, cannibalization versus net-new, category entry points, working capital to give it a fair shot, and launch timing. Product-channel fit is scored on margin structure and unit economics per channel, since a $200 product cannot live in convenience stores that want $5.99 to $19.99. Not all boxes have to be ticked, only enough to justify the investment. His margin rule: "higher margin is more idiot-proof", with the caveat that structurally low-margin businesses are viable and simply demand far more operational rigor.
Sources: Andrew Faris, You're Working Hard On The Wrong Problem. Here's How To Tell., 2026-07-27; Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), One Packaging Tweak, $375K In Profit, 2026-06-18
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-045 · Effective TAM equals the number of category entry points: more distinct ways to talk about a product means more scalable creative reach
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Beyond raw headcount of buyers, TAM widens with the number of distinct value propositions (category entry points) a product supports; the Mars Man hormone supplement example spans brain health, heart health, training, and strength, giving many creative angles at once. A product with many entry points scales faster because message variety, the core creative lever, is structurally available; a niche product like a $200 baseball-glove wallet caps out regardless of execution.

Size TAM per channel, never in aggregate. Bhogal runs the sequence category TAM, then channel-reachable TAM, then in-market-right-now TAM, and reports that a "couple billion dollar" category TAM can shrink 95% once restricted to what actually sells over a website to people in market at the right time, often landing at a couple million people. That final number is what sets the account's real spend ceiling. Operator move: when an account plateaus, back out the channel-restricted TAM before blaming creative. Reasoning only, no worked numbers shown.

Partial counter from Matt Shiver, and the tension is worth keeping rather than smoothing over: he holds that ONE pain point can carry a business to seven figures provided TAM is a million people or more. "If you have a big enough TAM you can ride one pain point all the way to seven figures." That cuts against the entry-point argument above, which says scale comes from the NUMBER of distinct ways you can talk about the product. Both can hold at different altitudes, since seven figures is well inside the ceiling a single-angle product hits, and the entry-point argument bites above it. Asserted, no data, and the million-person threshold is stated without derivation.
Sources: Andrew Faris, You're Working Hard On The Wrong Problem. Here's How To Tell., 2026-07-27; Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), One Packaging Tweak, $375K In Profit, 2026-06-18; Matt Shiver, The Exact Facebook Ad Testing System I Use to Scale Clients Past $100k/mo, 2026-07-07
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-046 · Large TAM tends to compress margin through competition, except where price signals value
Tier: T4 · Status: active
When a market is huge and the product is simple to make, entry floods in and drives price and gross margin down (backpacks: expensive zippers, crowded category, low LTV, hard differentiation). The exception is categories like beauty where pricing low signals a bad product, so high margin persists regardless of TAM. Crowded "perfect" categories (supplements) also bid up CAC until unit economics break at scale.
Sources: Andrew Faris, You're Working Hard On The Wrong Problem. Here's How To Tell., 2026-07-27
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-047 · A profitable owner-operated brand has almost no acquisition value; a barely-profitable brand that grows self-reliantly with a full team is worth far more
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A $100k/mo revenue brand where the founder does everything is nearly unsellable (some sold for maybe $20-40k) because the buyer acquires dependence, not an asset. A $100k/mo brand making only $5k/mo profit but with systems, team, and fulfillment that grow without the founder is materially more valuable. Second value driver: a genuinely new mechanism/product in a category already growing fast (his example: the first greens gummy, sold for over a billion dollars).
Sources: Nick Theriot, You Just Found a Winning Ad… Here's Exactly What To Do Next, 2026-07-31
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-048 · A declining business cannot realistically be rescued by an agency inside 30 days; turnarounds take 60-90 days if possible at all
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Client-selection floor: Shopify store doing at least $100k/month revenue, spending at least $30k/month on Facebook ads, with a positive P&L. For a business declining for 8 months, saving it within 30 days is "very unrealistic"; it takes 60-90 days "and that's even if we can do it." Creative and landing-page systems compound over time, so results need a solvent runway.
Sources: Nick Theriot, My Full Process For Scaling Facebook Ad Clients In 2026, 2026-07-20
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-049 · Hard-seasonal, giftable, and custom products concentrate nearly all annual revenue in the peak window (up to 500:1) and cannot be forced to scale off-season
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Beach umbrella client: roughly $1,000-10,000/month most of the year, then ~$100k in May, ~$500k in June, ~$700k in July, collapsing in August. Toy client: $5-30k/month baseline, then ~$300k October, ~$2-2.5M November, ~$1M December ($3.5-4M combined Q4), collapsing to ~$500/day in January; the client bought inventory expecting $4M in January, tried to force scale, and failed. He refuses seasonal, custom/personalized, and gifting-driven products as year-round businesses because performance exists only 2-3 times a year, making continuous testing and scaling impossible. He retold the umbrella case on 2026-06-19 with the figures slightly different, "they literally struggled to do $1,000 a month in revenue. And then June and July, they'll do like half a million dollars a month, each month. And then August, they fall all the way back down to like $1,000 a month," so June and July are both about $500k rather than $500k and $700k, and the off-season floor is $1,000 rather than $1,000 to $10,000. Recalled figures, not exports, and the spread between his two tellings is the measure of their precision. The shape is the claim; the exact months are not.

The fix is a counter-seasonal product, not marketing effort. Bhogal describes a women's apparel brand that died every summer and launched conservative high-coverage swimsuits, which turned summer into one of its stronger seasons and raised LTV, despite the new line having little to do with the core assortment. Faris states the choice explicitly: either lean into making the big season bigger, or go solve the low season with product. Pushing marketing spend at the trough wastes time and money. ASSERTED, no revenue-by-month data shown.

The same concentration shows up in service businesses, where the revenue recognises evenly but the CONTRACT is signed in one window. Professor Charley T puts over 75% of a membership gym's new-customer revenue in the 30 days after Christmas. His mechanism is commitment behaviour at the turn of the year: the same buyer who would only take a one-month trial in June signs a twelve-month contract in January because the annual price is lower per month. Operator consequence for any subscription or membership offer, the annual-commitment version of the offer belongs in that window and the trial version belongs the rest of the year. The 75% figure is asserted with no source or data shown.

Inside a gifting peak, the peak is set by a SHIPPING DEADLINE rather than by the holiday. Theriot reports 5 to 7 February, the days before the last shipping date for Valentine's Day, as the biggest days of the year across his gifting clients, six figures a day in revenue and the highest conversion rate of the year, because a buyer with no gift yet stops comparing and takes the first acceptable option. Operator consequence: for a gifting product the budget peak and the strongest offer belong on the last-ship window, not on the holiday itself, and the days after it are dead regardless of spend. Recalled across repeated years and multiple clients, no account data shown.
Sources: Nick Theriot, Simple Creative Testing Framework I Use To Scale Facebook Ads In 2026, 2026-08-12; Nick Theriot, The Most Braindead Way To Find Winning Products in 2026 (this will get banned), 2026-08-17; Nick Theriot, $30k days with Facebook Ads using Emotional Intensity, 2026-07-22; Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), One Packaging Tweak, $375K In Profit, 2026-06-18; Professor Charley T, Q5: the 2nd Black Friday: Meta Ads for Profit, 2025-12-20; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15; Nick Theriot, Your Ads Results Are Doomed If You Don't Do This Now!!!, 2026-06-19
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### MM-107 · Every launch already carries a stack of unknown variables, so never put an unproven product on top of unproven creative
Tier: T4 · Status: active
The variables in play at launch are media buying, creative, copy, product, market and algorithm. Creative is the largest single one and the one you can least control. If the product has never been proven to sell, a failed test cannot be attributed to anything, so nothing is learned and the money buys no information. The prescription is to enter markets where people are demonstrably already buying and hold the product constant while creative varies. This sits in tension with [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-041|MM-041]] ("winning products are created by marketing, not found"), and the reconciliation is that marketing creates the win only once demand for the category is already proven. Reasoning, no data.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-108 · Product development obeys venture math: 8 failures in 10 is a good outcome, and one portfolio brand missed roughly 20-22 times before the launch that paid for all of them
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Keep the product hopper permanently full, because the development cycle runs 1-3 years and an empty hopper is a multi-year growth gap no marketing can close. He also warns that a single successful launch is usually luck rather than evidence of product-development skill. Opex should be deliberately allocated toward product bets the same way it is allocated toward creative volume. Faris generalizes it: run product, creative production and offers as portfolios of bets, and size each bet against the win it could produce. The gate is cost-of-shot relative to upside rather than probability of success, so he will spend more and wait longer on a product that can live in four channels at good margin, and refuses expensive shots at barely-incremental SKUs. ASSERTED from portfolio experience, no data shown.

Faris adds the marketing-side reason product development is not optional, calling it the fourth cylinder of the engine. Tactically, limited edition drops, flavours, colourways and influencer collabs are what give the promotional calendar new material to work with (see [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-145|MM-145]]), and they generate LTV from the super-fan segment that disproportionately drives retention revenue. Strategically, it is the answer when the core category is saturated or simply returns less per unit of effort, his example being Ridge nurturing an adjacent category. He grants the counter-case honestly: single-SKU brands that cut product development entirely and redirect that energy into supply chain redundancy, supplier terms and COGS gain economies of scale faster. Apparel and jewellery brands have no such choice. Reasoning, no data shown.
Sources: Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), One Packaging Tweak, $375K In Profit, 2026-06-18; Andrew Faris, The 4 Cylinder Marketing Engine Powering My $10M+ Ecom Clients' Success, 2026-05-04
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-109 · Marketing cannot push past the local maximum set by the existing product portfolio; past that ceiling only new products that expand TAM raise growth at target CAC and margin
Tier: T4 · Status: active
The diagnostic is whether spend can grow at target CAC. If it cannot, and creative, offer and landing-page work have already been done, the constraint is the product portfolio and the conversation belongs with whoever owns product. This converts "our ads stopped scaling" into a portfolio question. It also gives the codex a stopping rule for tactical scaling and creative claims: they are all bounded by the portfolio. Reasoning only, no data.

A concrete 30-to-60-day test for the same diagnosis, from Austin at Grow My Ads on an over-segmented Google account. Ignore the ROAS goal, lump the brands or products that carried most of last year's volume into a single Maximize Conversion Value PMax campaign, and let it run. If the account cannot convert on its own proven baseline within 30 to 60 days, the problem is product market, website or positioning, not campaign structure. The transferable part is the design: point the simplest possible campaign at the historically proven demand and use failure there as evidence about the business rather than the account. Applied to one consulting case, no outcome data reported.
Sources: Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), One Packaging Tweak, $375K In Profit, 2026-06-18; Blue Sense Digital, The Best Google Ad Structure in eCommerce (ft Austin from Grow My Ads), 2025-02-27
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-110 · Visible agency and operator "marketing win" stories are largely client selection, so reverse-engineering a tactic from an outcome is unreliable
Tier: T4 · Status: active
The claim the codex should carry because it sets the discount rate on every T3 practitioner claim inside it. Bhogal's case: outdoor and fitness operators looked like 300-IQ marketers in 2020-21 on demand that COVID pulled forward, and the same operators cannot make those categories work now. The winners largely picked clients that already had product-market fit and rode the growth. He applies it to himself too, saying it is very hard to know why something worked even for your own brands. Operator move: before copying a tactic, check whether the source's product, margin and TAM conditions match yours.
Sources: Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), One Packaging Tweak, $375K In Profit, 2026-06-18
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-111 · In a two-sided resale marketplace only 13% of sellers also buy, and the two sides are opposite demographics, so one brand has to run two structurally different acquisition motions
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Fashionphile's sellers skew older and wealthier and mostly shop the primary market rather than resale; buyers skew younger and treat resale as a badge of honour. A 13% crossover kills the cheap assumption that you can monetize one side with the other. Supply is the constrained side, which inverts the usual e-commerce assumption that demand generation is the hard part. Operator translation for any marketplace or trade-in model: split budget and creative by side, and build the seller-side offer around speed and certainty of payment rather than price. Founder-reported figure, not shown.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Fashionphile's 27 Year Journey To $400M, 2026-05-29
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-112 · Competitor awareness spending can erase your category mindshare while your own numbers still grow
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Fashionphile was named by Entrepreneur Magazine in 2010 as one of the 100 smartest, most innovative companies, and was still bootstrapped, profitable and growing when The RealReal raised over $300M pre-IPO. The founder's read is that share of voice moved, not product quality or profitability: they kept growing while going under the radar for years and went from owning the awareness in their category to effectively unknown. It was the primary trigger for taking outside capital in 2018-2019 after 20 years bootstrapped, alongside technology and hiring people who had operated at larger scale. Awareness share is relative and can be bought away from you while your own trajectory looks fine, which is a real caution against judging brand health from your own numbers alone. Anecdotal, no share-of-voice measurement shown.

Theriot puts a lag on the product version of the same problem: "until one day you see a new competitor start running ads. Their product was better in every way... The first 30 days, no impact. 90 days later and it's stealing your market share." Ninety days between a better-funded or better-product competitor launching and the damage arriving in your account. If the number is even roughly right it has a reporting consequence: a performance decline you are diagnosing today may have been caused by something that entered the auction a quarter ago, and no creative or structure change made in that window is the cause. Narrative, no data, and 90 days is offered as a feel rather than a measurement.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Fashionphile's 27 Year Journey To $400M, 2026-05-29; Nick Theriot, The 7 Levels of Scaling Facebook Ads, 2026-06-24
Last touched: 2026-08-20

## US Market Entry Economics

### MM-050 · US CPMs run roughly 2x a small home market, so copy-pasting home campaigns into the US doubles CAC at equal CTR and CVR
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Average CPMs cited: Australia ~$11 vs US ~$22. If CTR and conversion rate stay the same, doubled CPMs double cost per acquisition, which breaks most brands' unit economics on entry. Markets financially weaker than Australia (UK, NZ, much of Eastern Europe) have even lower CPMs, widening the gap. Stated from managing ~$300M/yr in spend (50% US) across 20+ US launches.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The US Launch Playbook for Non-US Brands, 2026-08-17
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-051 · The correct offset for higher US CPMs is price, not better CTR/CVR: reprice up 50-60% against the home-market price
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Average order value data cited: Australia ~$103 vs US ~$151, about 50% higher, which he says explains most of the CPM gap. Everyone tries to out-optimize CTR and CVR instead; the structural fix is pricing to the US market's AOV norm so dollars-per-advertising-dollar match the competitors who are outbidding you.

The practical form for Australian brands is 1:1 currency, not conversion: sell at $70 USD what sells at $70 AUD. Converting $70 AUD down to about $52 USD hands the US dollar's higher purchasing power back to the customer. Fashion-specific companions: do not expand below roughly A$10M/yr (consistent with [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-052|MM-052]]), launch with proven local winners rather than fresh creative, expect low-fi content to outperform in the US relative to AU, filter the catalogue to trans-seasonal product where there is no Northern Hemisphere buying, and pick states on weather and demographics. Case: an in-house team with four to five evergreen winners each carrying roughly $250k of lifetime AU spend exported them to the US, got better efficiency there than at home because the AU market was fatigued, and roughly tripled the business, with the US now half of revenue.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The US Launch Playbook for Non-US Brands, 2026-08-17; Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-052 · Do not launch into the US below $3M USD/yr in the home market; ideal threshold is $10M+
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Revenue level is a proxy for operator skill; a $3M operator competes against generally stronger US businesses. Brands doing $10M+ locally that delayed the launch consistently crush it because they arrive with skills and a bank of proven winning ads. For a new-country launch, model gross profit on first purchase only (no LTV credit) and require profitability on entry so the launch is not capital-intensive.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The US Launch Playbook for Non-US Brands, 2026-08-17
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-053 · Advertising yourself as a foreign brand tanks US conversion rates; full localization (perceived-US brand) is required
Tier: T3 · Status: active
US buyers avoid international-framed brands due to tariff exposure, shipping-time fear, and currency/customs friction, so "proudly Australian" branding backfires; everything on landing pages and ads should read as a US brand. Related economics: hold local US inventory because tariffs are paid on cost-of-goods, not retail price (de minimis suspension ended direct dropshipping; tariffs lowered/capped after a Feb 2026 Supreme Court ruling but surcharges remain). US fashion return rates can run up to 2x Australia, which must reduce both LTGP and the new-customer count in the LTV:CAC calc.

The same operator brackets that 2x differently in an earlier session: 50% to 100% higher for fashion brands entering the US, with some clients running a 3x refund rate against their home market. So his own range across two sessions is 1.5x to 3x, and 2x sits in the middle of it rather than being the number. Treat the multiple as unmeasured and pull your own. Full note at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-072|MM-072]].
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The US Launch Playbook for Non-US Brands, 2026-08-17; Blue Sense Digital, How To Breakdown Shopify Data Into Country Unit Economics with Valentin, 2025-07-22
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-166 · Country-level unit economics: rank markets by profit contribution times orders, not by per-unit economics, and expect the higher-AOV country to be the lower-gross-profit country
Tier: T4 · Status: active
BANK THE METHOD, REFUSE EVERY NUMBER. The speaker screen-shares a filled model and states outright that the figures in it are altered: "the numbers in this template are fudged up, but they're based on real clients' results. I just didn't want to use real clients' results uh to protect their, you know, confidentiality." He also revises one on the fly during the walkthrough. Nothing in this source reaches T2 and no figure from it may ever be quoted as a benchmark, including in a client deck.

The method. Three prerequisites before a country breakdown is possible at all: cost of goods tracked in Shopify or an inventory system, third-party-logistics or carrier data, and marketing spend tracked by location. Without all three the exercise cannot be run and any country P&L is a guess. Country priority is then set by profit contribution multiplied by orders rather than by per-unit economics alone, so a country with worse unit economics is not switched off if it carries the volume that pays for the fixed base. A geography can be negative on new-customer acquisition and positive blended, and that combination is where the strategic conversation starts rather than where it ends.

Two structural findings that survive the fudged numbers, because they are mechanisms rather than figures. The higher-AOV country can be the LOWER gross-profit country once variable costs land, because shipping and fulfilment differ by geography and the AOV advantage is spent on freight. And a free-shipping threshold set globally can make a country lose money per order on shipping, covered at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-089|MM-089]]. Both say the same thing: AOV ranks nothing across borders. The return-rate leg of the same argument is at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-072|MM-072]] and the processing-fee leg at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-064|MM-064]].
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How To Breakdown Shopify Data Into Country Unit Economics with Valentin, 2025-07-22
Last touched: 2026-08-21

## Lead-Gen Funnel Rates

### MM-160 · A lead-gen KPI board read off screen: 75% show for business-to-business, 50-60% for consumer, 40% close against roughly 20% on cold traffic, 50%+ of contract value collected up front
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Show rate splits by audience type and he names the reason: "if you're meeting with a business owner who uses Google Calendar, the show rates are going to be higher... They're more bought in typically. But B2C, where you're selling mass market, you're going to have a 50%, 60% show rate." Close rate target is 40% or above against roughly 20% for cold traffic, and he credits the entire gap to front-end disqualification: "for a lot of cold traffic, you're going to probably start around I would say 20%. But for us, we're able to get to 40% because we disqualify so heavily in our application process". Cash collected target is 50% or more of contract value, driven by a 20% paid-in-full discount so acquisition cost is recovered on the front end: "if I can get the customer acquisition cost covered on the front end, I can reinvest that back into the business". The KPI board was SHOWN on screen. The rates on it are ASSERTED, with no export or cohort behind any of them.

Two things to hold against it. The disqualification claim reports a doubled close rate with no stated cost to lead volume, and a filter that halves lead flow to double close rate is a different business decision from one that is free. Nothing in the source measures the volume side, so treat 40% as a rate achieved at an unstated price. And the same operator's own worst-case forecast in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-016|MM-016]] uses 50% show and 10% close. His target close rate and his own honest worst case are 4x apart, which is the correct spread to carry into any forecast rather than either endpoint alone. Grading ads on the downstream close rate rather than on cost per lead is [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-082|MM-082]].
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, If I Wanted to Make My First 100k mo in 2026 Id Do This, 2026-06-02
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-161 · Page-level conversion rates for a call funnel: VSL with no opt-in in front converts about 5% to a booked call, an opt-in step in front puts 10-20% of opt-ins through to a booking, a free-community lead magnet opts in 25-30%
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The three page types were SHOWN on screen. The rates are ASSERTED with no analytics displayed. A VSL offer page with no opt-in gate converts roughly 5% of visitors straight into a booked call or submitted application. Put an opt-in step in front and 10 to 20% of the opt-ins reach the VSL page and then book or apply, so the second number is a rate on a smaller and warmer base than the first and the two are not directly comparable. A free-community lead magnet page opts in 25 to 30% of visitors, which is a page rate on the cheapest possible commitment and says nothing about what those opt-ins are worth.

Traffic temperature is ambiguous on the 5% figure, because he mentions warm traffic in the same breath. Do not carry it as a cold-traffic benchmark. The category-benchmark routing rule in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-106|MM-106]] applies before any of these are used to declare a page broken.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, If I Wanted to Make My First 100k mo in 2026 Id Do This, 2026-06-02
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-162 · A measured opt-in-to-response failure: 43 people into a funnel, 5 responses, 1 booked call, and an account audited sub-10% against an asserted 20-25% target
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Dashboard screenshot SHOWN. "from this just the screenshot, from this one funnel, 43 people went in, five people responded, one call got booked... This response rate should be 20 25% plus. And I audited it and it was sub 10." Keep the two halves visibly separate, because only one is measured. MEASURED: 43 opted-in leads produced 5 responses to outbound messages, which is 11.6%, and one booked call, which is 2.3% of opt-ins and 20% of responders. The account-level audit put response rate under 10%. ASSERTED: the 20 to 25% target, which has nothing behind it and no account named.

Why it matters for a lead-gen book. The cost per opt-in on a funnel like this can look fine while 9 in 10 opted-in leads never reply, so the whole loss sits between the platform metric and the calendar and is invisible in Ads Manager. Anyone reading cost per lead alone would have graded this funnel as working. The follow-up mechanism that carries the response is [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-163|MM-163]].
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, If I Wanted to Make My First 100k mo in 2026 Id Do This, 2026-06-02
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-163 · Double confirmation is what holds show rate: confirm on opt-in, confirm again 24 hours before, phone anyone who does not confirm
Tier: T3 · Status: active
"this is the thing that's helped us so much with show rate is to get double confirmation. Get it immediately when they opt in and then also again 24 hours beforehand. If they do not get it, pick up the phone and call them." ASSERTED, with no before-and-after show rate attached, so the size of the effect is unknown and only the mechanism transfers. The second half of the same system: every opted-in lead gets an SMS whose job is qualification and booking rather than nurture, and a setter working DMs, SMS and the community inbox carries a KPI of 1 to 2 booked calls per day.

The gap in the evidence is exactly the number an operator would want. He names double confirmation as the single biggest lever on his show rate and never states the rate before or after. Anyone testing it should log show rate for the four weeks before and after the second confirmation is added, because the confirmation step and the phone call are two changes shipped together. Show-rate targets by audience type sit at [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-160|MM-160]]. The human-versus-automation question on who sends these is [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-056|MM-056]].
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, If I Wanted to Make My First 100k mo in 2026 Id Do This, 2026-06-02
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-164 · Two DM-ad accounts where cheap leads sat next to a show rate that destroyed the economics: 50-cent leads and $10-20 booked calls at a 10% show rate
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Both accounts are own-account or client recall with nothing shown on screen. Account one: cost per message bottomed at $1.50, and $4 to $5 is his stated red-flag threshold. At $200 a day the account books 2 to 3 calls a day, which implies roughly $67 to $100 per booked call, and roughly 90% of the closes are the intended avatar. Account two: DM ads produced roughly 50-cent leads and $10 to $20 booked calls at about a 10% show rate, with the show rate explicitly hedged by the speaker. Her later webinar funnel ran a customer acquisition cost of about $144 against a $3,000-plus offer, read off her dashboard on the day.

The pattern across the two is the claim. A 50-cent lead and a $15 booked call are meaningless at a 10% show rate, because the real unit is $150 per held call, which is worse than account one's $67 to $100 per booked call before its show rate is even applied. Cheap upstream metrics sitting next to a collapsed show rate is the same failure shape as [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-162|MM-162]], one step further down the funnel. Never grade a DM-ad account on cost per message or cost per booked call without the show rate attached.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How Francis Scaled His Fitness Business with Instagram Ads, 2026-04-30; Dr. Matt Shiver, How One Coach Dominates a Niche With Almost No Competition, 2026-04-23
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-165 · A 12,000-person free community and a 385-person paid community produced identical response to the same post on the same morning: 36 hand raises each
Tier: T2 · Status: active
A clean comparison with a defined control, which is rare in this corpus. "I have 12,000 people in a free community. I have 385 people in a paid community. I ran the same post in both this morning and it is the exact same engagement... 12,000 people, 36 hand raises. 385 paying people, 36". Same post, same morning, both figures named. Stated verbally with nothing shown on screen, so the numbers are his recall of a comparison he ran rather than an export.

He does not compute the rates. They are 0.3% against 9.4%, roughly 31x. The operating consequence for any lead-gen book: headcount is not a measure of list value. A list assembled at zero commitment responds at a rate that makes the headcount meaningless, so audience size can only be read alongside how the audience was acquired. It also puts a price on the free-community opt-in rate of 25 to 30% in [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-161|MM-161]]: the cheapest opt-in on the board buys the least responsive person on the board.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, Two 7-Figure Coaches Compare Their Skool Funnels, 2026-06-11
Last touched: 2026-08-21

## Team and Org Economics

### MM-054 · One full-time closer per $50-100k of monthly revenue; part-time closers fail because their incentive is to take calls, not work the pipeline
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A closer on ~10% commission earns $5-10k/mo at that ratio, which sustains a full-time A player. Fractional closers who share calendars across businesses only show up for booked calls and skip follow-ups and pipeline work. His business has done $200k+/mo with one setter and one closer by strictly managing the calendar; three or four closers at under $100k/mo means the closers are bad.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How I Built a $2.25M/Year Coaching Business (Copy My System), 2026-08-11
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-055 · B2C coaching needs roughly twice the fulfillment headcount of B2B at the same revenue because B2B price points run about 2x higher
Tier: T3 · Status: active
B2B offers carry a financial outcome so they price ~2x higher, meaning fewer clients per revenue dollar and a smaller delivery team. He cites clients at $50-100k/mo whose team complexity is twice his own purely because their offer price is half. B2B at $20-50k/mo typically needs one fulfillment hire; B2C needs at least two coaches at the same stage.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How I Built a $2.25M/Year Coaching Business (Copy My System), 2026-08-11
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-056 · AI booking bots only pay off with high lead flow; at low volume every lead needs a human setter
Tier: T3 · Status: active
At the $0-20k/mo stage he recommends a human messenger (20 hours/week, ~4 hours/day, booking about one call a day) over AI booking bots, even though his company sells such software, because with few leads each one is too valuable to risk on automation. He also found two 20-hour setters outperform one 40-hour setter because quality diminishes on long solo shifts.

He went further on 2026-06-02 and reported a reversion, not a stage preference. His account tried AI booking and went back to a person: "We tried some of the um AI booking bot stuff, and it just didn't work as well for us. So, our current process is still a human setter in there." ASSERTED, with no comparison numbers on either side, no lead volume stated, and no window. Loosely corroborated by the same operator elsewhere describing an AI setter and closer bot that "put too much pressure on me to be on like the one who's QCing it", which is an operator-load complaint rather than a performance result. The setter KPI he runs the human against, from the same session: 1 to 2 booked calls per day across DMs, SMS and the community inbox, which is the higher end of the "about one call a day" figure above.

Read this as an outside operator reporting a negative result with nothing measured. It argues directly against replacing a setter with an AI voice agent, so name what would settle it rather than accepting or dismissing it: the same lead source split down the middle for one month, human on one half and agent on the other, graded on booked calls per opted-in lead and on show rate, not on response speed. Nothing in the source does that. The double-confirmation mechanism the setter is executing is [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-163|MM-163]], and it is machine-executable, which is where an agent would have to win.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How I Built a $2.25M/Year Coaching Business (Copy My System), 2026-08-11; Dr. Matt Shiver, If I Wanted to Make My First 100k mo in 2026 Id Do This, 2026-06-02
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### MM-057 · People-cost benchmark for e-commerce at scale: 5% of revenue on people (including agencies) is good, 3% exceptional
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A cited brand runs at 3% of revenue on people including agencies while scaling from ~$3M to $8M/month in a month and projecting $12-15M/month within 2 months. 5% is called "good" and 3% "exceptional"; the low ratio is partly a function of scale since opex scales sublinearly. With a cost base that good, chasing more AI adoption is a distraction.
Sources: Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), The AI Traps Wasting Your Time In An Ecommerce Brand, 2026-08-03
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-058 · Do not automate any task a $600-$1,200/month offshore employee can do unless it is mission-critical; build automations fast with a human in the loop
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Automating entry-level offshore work saves essentially no money. Fully automated pipelines cost far more time than human-in-the-loop, which was "100x faster to build" and more reliable; optimize for build speed over robustness because base models improve so much every ~6 months that hardened pipelines become obsolete. Never vibe-code mission-critical software (ERP, Klaviyo-class ESP); the hard part is the reliable back end, so buy it.
Sources: Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), The AI Traps Wasting Your Time In An Ecommerce Brand, 2026-08-03
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-059 · AI's structural payoff in e-commerce orgs is deleting the management layer: one $200-220k doer replaces roughly three $80k roles
Tier: T3 · Status: active
AI multiplies individual-contributor output so the manager layer between C-level and doers becomes mostly unnecessary: replace three people at ~$80k each with one at $200-220k doing the equivalent of many. The 80/20 of AI impact is opex reduction and amplifying an already-working process (e.g. creative output), not inserting AI where it is not a natural fit; a business already growing fast should mostly ignore the AI shiny object.
Sources: Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), The AI Traps Wasting Your Time In An Ecommerce Brand, 2026-08-03
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-060 · An agency's growth constraint is people and service quality, not cash: service businesses cash-flow without inventory, unlike e-commerce where cash is the scaling dragon
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Scaling an e-commerce brand is primarily cash-constrained (funding inventory ahead of sales); scaling an ad agency is constrained by founder bandwidth, hiring quality (growth strategists and creative strategists are the outsized value-creation roles and hardest to find), and quality-of-service degradation risk. AJF planned 200% growth for the year and is tracking ~450%, betting that relentless service quality is the fastest long-run growth path via churn reduction and word of mouth.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Should Our Ad Agency Grow Slower? (With Patrick Coddou), 2026-08-17
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-061 · On a complex Shopify store, a botched release costs $20-40k per day in lost conversions even when caught within 2 hours
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Dev-heavy stores (metafield workarounds, multi-item checkout) have had releases costing roughly $20-40k/day in conversion drop-off despite a team that catches issues in about two hours. Their fix is AI-driven adversarial QC: an in-house dev has the model write adversarial reviews of themes pre-release, which sharply reduced incidents. Related: Shopify checkout degrades noticeably past 5-8 items per cart, so 12-item-average carts see far more release and site-speed damage.
Sources: Andrew Faris (with Mehtab Bhogal), The AI Traps Wasting Your Time In An Ecommerce Brand, 2026-08-03
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-114 · Creative production budget should be roughly 25% of media budget at $30-100k/month spend, asymptoting toward 10% above $100k/month
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Below $30k/month the production budget is founder time rather than money, and statics plus founder-shot video are enough. At $30-100k/month, 25% of media budget goes into production, so $25k/month at $100k of spend. He calls the 25% band deliberately uncomfortable and says it is "way more than like 99% of brands are actually spending", naming under-allocation here as a primary reason accounts stall. Above $100k/month it settles toward 10%, which is roughly 2.5% of revenue at a 25% MER, and at that level funds UGC, partnership ads, a designer for statics, and a low-cost agency for one additional format. This is a separate and larger line item than [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-022|MM-022]] (about 5% on better operators, about 10% on creator partnership ads).
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-115 · Cost to produce one asset must be under the average contribution margin per ad unit, calculated per format, and most brands are underwater without knowing it
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The inputs: mean lifetime spend per ad unit runs about $700 to $1,500 depending on the account, and mean revenue per ad about $2,000, both from their audit process. Subtract cost of goods and the cost of serving the ad and you get contribution margin per ad unit, roughly $350 in his example. If producing one ad costs $400, the creative machine is destroying value, and the fix is either cheaper formats or a better hit rate. Run the same calculation per format to decide the production mix. Operator action: compute your own mean spend per ad from the last 12 months (total spend divided by ads launched) rather than borrowing his number, because everything downstream keys off it, then track cost per asset by source and read ROI at individual-ad level as spend against production cost.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-116 · Creative strategists are commonly paid roughly 3% of the ad spend their creatives generate, on top of base salary, and the model breaks at large scale
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The incentive is direct: if their ads do not take spend, they do not get paid, so hit rate is what they optimize. He reports it works well on mid-size brands and becomes untenable on large accounts, where the payout can reach about $80,000/month and companies move off the model. Hiring guidance attached: the role barely existed 2-3 years ago, so source from copywriters (creative strategy is mostly copywriting, meaning scripts, static briefs and persona ideation), poach an existing strategist, or train up a designer or video editor, which tightens the feedback loop because they can ideate and produce.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### MM-137 · Two 2025 datasets found no measured business benefit from AI adoption: 192 profitable e-com brands saw adopters grow net income 33% against 55% for non-adopters
Tier: T2 · Status: active
The E-commerce Fuel 2025 trends report from Andrew Youderian, controlled to profitable businesses at $1M to $25M revenue with under 100 employees, n=192. Revenue growth 27% for self-described AI adopters against 28% for non-adopters, effectively tied. Net income growth 33% for adopters against 55% for non-adopters. Revenue per employee roughly $830k for adopters against $930k for non-adopters, about $100k lower, with non-adopters carrying one more employee on average. One result runs the other way and has to be reported: net profit MARGIN was higher for adopters, so only the growth rate favoured non-adopters.

Second data point, weaker. Brent Beshore's 2025 annual letter reports placing a PhD technologist over 16 portfolio companies specifically to drive AI adoption and seeing no impact across the board, and quotes a large PE firm founder who publicly promotes an AI programme and privately called its value "absolutely nothing. It's window dressing."

Both readings are disputed on stream. Faris rejects the generalisation from his own agency experience. Coddou rejects the survey outright as stale, placing the step change in AI capability at the end of December 2025, and notes Beshore's portfolio skews to manufacturing and other non-tech-native businesses. Self-described adopter status makes the survey observational, not a controlled test, and there is an obvious selection story running the other way, that struggling businesses adopt tools looking for a fix. The higher net margin for adopters is broadly consistent with [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-059|MM-059]] (AI's payoff is opex reduction), while the lower net income growth is not, so read the two together rather than treating either as settled.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Why Are Early AI Adopters' Businesses Performing So Badly? (With Patrick Coddou), 2026-05-08
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-138 · The $1,500 to $2,000 monthly Meta retainer no longer funds what the platform requires, so the price floor for sub-$15k/month spenders has risen
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Austin from Grow My Ads argues that fulfilling on modern Meta delivery requires a creative team rather than campaign management, and that team cost cannot be recovered from the $1,500 to $2,000 retainers common on small accounts. His conclusion: the cost of entry rises for advertisers spending $10k to $15k a month or less, and falls for large spenders, because everyone now runs a similar framework and the differentiation moved to creative volume. Both speakers agree low-retainer button-clicking Meta management is finished and will be replaced by software. Austin spent three to four months hiring strategists, creatives and video editors to build the capability rather than bolting on generic management.

This is the agency-side statement of [[Marketing Math & Unit Economics#MM-114|MM-114]] (creative production at 25% of media budget at $30-100k/month) and it produces an uncomfortable arithmetic for small accounts: a brand spending $10k/month would need roughly $2,500 of production on top of the retainer, which is more than the retainer itself. Practitioner claim from agency pricing work, no cost model shown.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Google Ads in 2026 (w/ Austin from Grow My Ads), 2025-12-15
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### MM-139 · Fund creative production as a fixed monthly floor you are required to spend, because ad-distribution losses are invisible and creative losses are legible
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Faris's thesis: operators are too comfortable wasting money on ad distribution and not comfortable enough wasting it on creative production, and the correct allocation is the reverse. Below-target ad spend usually has no upside path attached, so calling it risk-taking is wrong, while creative production spend has genuinely variable upside and deserves aggressive funding. His test for whether below-target ad spend is acceptable is whether the brand has a structured way to measure the impact of those dollars over time, which he says most do not.

The behavioural mechanism he gives for the misallocation is the billing surface. Meta bills the whole account as one monthly credit line, so a $160,000 bill covering a 2x campaign, a second 2x campaign and a 1.2x campaign arrives as a single undifferentiated charge with no per-campaign return attached. A creator paid a couple of thousand dollars produces a discrete invoice, so a poor result is legible and gets cancelled. Invisible losses persist, visible ones get killed, and the size of the loss has nothing to do with which survives.
